under Alternatives

In this section, we will first establish CLTs for
\hat{\eta}
under a fixed alternative and under certain shrinking alternatives converging to the null. Using these CLTs, we characterize the asymptotic power of the test based on
\hat{\eta}
. In particular, this provides the detection threshold of the test based on
\hat{\eta}
. We will focus on
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
d\geq 1
) in this section, and assume that
P_{i}
has a density
f_{i}
w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure, for all
i=1,\ldots,M
.

The asymptotic distribution of geometric graph-based statistics under alternatives and their detection thresholds were not available until the recent work of
Bhattacharya 2020
,
where the analysis was carried out under a Poissonized setting.
The spatial independence of the Poisson process facilitates the computation of the asymptotic variance of
\hat{\eta}
, and also helps establish the CLTs for
\hat{\eta}
using Stein’s method
[
18
]
.
We will also consider this Poissonized framework in this section.
Although de-Poissonization techniques are available in the literature
[
52
,
54
]
, the de-Poissonized version of our theorem (Theorem
B.1
) unfortunately does not reduce to our original setting in the main paper where the sample size
n_{i}
’s are nonrandom.

In the Poissonized framework, instead of assuming
\frac{n_{i}}{n}\to\pi_{i}
, it is assumed that
n_{i}\sim{\rm Poisson}(N_{i})
, for
i=1,\ldots,M
.
Write
N:=\sum_{i=1}^{M}N_{i}
and assume
\frac{N_{i}}{N}\to\pi_{i}>0

as
N\to\infty
.
An equivalent characterization is that we first decide to draw in total
n\sim{\rm Poisson}(N)
data points, and for each data point, with probability
\frac{N_{i}}{N}
, we draw an observation from the
i
-th population, for
i=1,\ldots,M
.
In the following, we provide CLTs for
\hat{\eta}
under fixed and shrinking alternatives, under the Poissonized setting when we let
N\to\infty
.

B.1
Asymptotic Normality under a General Fixed Alternative

In the following, we briefly sketch how a CLT for
\hat{\eta}
, under a fixed alternative, can be obtained after proper centering; see Theorem
B.1
for the precise statement.
Since the Poisson distribution is tightly concentrated around its mean:
{\rm Var}\left(\frac{n_{i}}{N_{i}}\right)=\frac{1}{N_{i}}\to 0
as
N_{i}\to\infty
. Thus, we have
\frac{n_{i}}{N_{i}}\overset{p}{\to}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{n_{i}}{N_{i}}\right]=1
.
Hence the denominator of
\hat{\eta}
in (
4
) converges in probability to
\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}K(i,i)-\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}K(i,j)>0
. So our analysis focuses on the asymptotic behavior of the numerator in (
4
):

\tilde{H}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}).

When the above quantity cannot be well-defined, i.e.,
n=0
or
1
, or
n\leq k
when a
k
-NN graph is used, we set
\tilde{H}_{n}=\hat{\eta}=0
.
Using
U
-statistics projection theory
[
21
, Lemma D.4]
, it can be shown that
n\tilde{H}_{n}/\sqrt{N}
has the same asymptotic distribution as
H_{n}
defined as

H_{n}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{N}(\Delta_{i}),

which has a non-degenerate asymptotic normal distribution after centering by its mean.
Here,

g_{N}(\Delta_{i}):=2\sum_{p=1}^{M}\frac{N_{p}}{N}K(\Delta_{i},p)-\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}\frac{N_{p}N_{q}}{N^{2}}K(p,q).

Similar to the CLT result under
{\rm H}_{0}
(see Theorem
3
), if we let
\mathcal{F}_{n}:=\sigma(n,Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
, the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the unlabelled data and the number of total observations,
then
H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})
given
\mathcal{F}_{n}
converges in distribution to a normal limit
N(0,\kappa_{1}^{2})
(see Theorem
B.1
for the exact expression for
\kappa_{1}^{2}
). We can also show that the unconditional distribution of
\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})
converges to another normal limit
N(0,\kappa_{2}^{2})
; see Theorem
B.1
for the exact expression for
\kappa_{2}^{2}
. Finally, a simple argument using characteristic functions shows that
H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2})
.

If
{\rm H}_{0}
holds, then it can be shown that
\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})=\mathbb{E}(H_{n})=0
, and
\kappa_{1}^{2}
is exactly the distribution-free variance derived in Theorem
4
, and
\kappa_{2}^{2}=0
.
However, in a general situation,
\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})
is a function of
\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}\}
, which follows a non-homogeneous Poisson process
8
8

8

For a non-homogeneous Poisson process with intensity function
f
(denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{f}
), the number of points in a set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
follows a

{\rm Poisson}(\int_{A}f(x){\rm d}x)
distribution, and the number of points in disjoint sets are independent.

\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
under the Poissonized setting, with

\phi_{N}(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{N_{i}}{N}f_{i}(z)\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\phi(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}f_{i}(z),\quad\mbox{for }\;z\in\mathcal{Z},

(B.1)

being the marginal density of
Z
given the total number of observations, and its limiting value, respectively.
To show the convergence of the variance of
\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})
and establish a CLT for
\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})
using Stein’s method,
we will need to make another common assumption — the
power-law stabilization

[
55
,
54
,
11
]
— on the non-homogeneous Poisson process
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
(in addition to the stabilization on the homogeneous Poisson process
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}
defined in Section
4.2
),
which will be introduced below.

Recall our setup:
\mathcal{G}
is a geometric graph on
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{X}
is a set of points in
\mathcal{Z}
.
\mathcal{E}\left(x,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X})\right)
is the set of
edges in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X})
that are incident to
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
Let
A
be a set with
\phi
-probability 1, e.g.,
A={\rm supp}(\phi)
, the support of
\phi
. Fix
x\in\mathcal{Z}
. A

radius of stabilization

R\equiv R(N,x)
at
x
exists if the points in
A\backslash B(x,N^{-1/d}R)
cannot impact the neighborhood of
x
in
\mathcal{G}\left(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x}\right)
where
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x}:=\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}\cup\{x\}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{E}\left(x,\mathcal{G}\left([\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x}\cap B(x,N^{-1/d}R)]\cup\mathcal{X}\right)\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(x,\mathcal{G}\left(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x}\cap B(x,N^{-1/d}R)\right)\right),

(B.2)

for all finite
\mathcal{X}\subset A\backslash B(x,N^{-1/d}R)
. Such a finite
R
always exists when
A
is bounded.
For certain graphs such as a
k
-NN graph, the
power-law stabilization
states that
R
is bounded in probability under mild assumptions, and the tail probability decays sufficiently fast, uniformly over
N
and
x
. To define it formally, let

\tau(t):=\sup_{N\geq 1,x\in A}\mathbb{P}[R(N,x)>t],\qquad\mbox{for}\;\;t>0.

(B.3)

Then,
\mathcal{G}
is said to be:

1.

power-law stabilizing
of order
q
with respect to
\phi_{N}
if
\sup_{t\geq 1}t^{q}\tau(t)<\infty
,

2.

exponentially stabilizing
with respect to
\phi_{N}
if
\limsup_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\log\tau(t)<0
.

Note that if
\mathcal{G}
is exponentially stabilizing, then it is power-law stabilizing for all
q>0
.
It is known that a
k
-NN graph is exponentially stabilizing under suitable conditions (see Proposition
B.1
below and Appendix
C.11
for its proof).
Other exponentially stabilizing graphs include the
Voronoi and Delaunay graphs
[
54
,
11
]
.
However, there is no similar tail bounds known for MST, as far as we are aware.

Proposition B.1

(
Penrose 2007
)
.

Assume
\phi_{N}
is bounded away from 0 on its support which is assumed to be convex. Then the
k
-NN graph (with
k
fixed, either directed or undirected) is exponentially stabilizing.

Assuming that the graph
\mathcal{G}
is power-law stabilizing of sufficient order w.r.t. our data generating process
\phi_{N}
, the asymptotic normality of
\hat{\eta}
can be established, as shown below.

Theorem B.1

(CLT under a fixed alternative)
.

Suppose that we have the Poissonized setting where we have
n_{i}\sim{\rm Poisson}(N_{i})
samples from the
i
-th distribution, for
i=1,\ldots,M
. Suppose the following assumptions hold:

1.

\sqrt{N}\left(\frac{N_{i}}{N}-\pi_{i}\right)\to 0
as
N\to\infty
, for
i=1,\ldots,M
, where
N=\sum_{i=1}^{M}N_{i}
.

2.

The
i
-th distribution has a Lebesgue density
f_{i}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which is Lebesgue almost everywhere continuous, for
i=1,\ldots,M
.

3.

\phi:=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}f_{i}
is bounded above and has a bounded support.

4.

\mathcal{G}
is translation and scale invariant, stabilizing on
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}
for some
\lambda>0
.

5.

\mathcal{G}
is power-law stabilizing w.r.t.
\phi_{N}
(see (
B.1
)) with order
q_{0}>16d
,
with the corresponding full probability set
A
and radius of stabilization
R
as defined in (
B.2
).

6.

The degree bound (including in- and out-degrees) for
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})
is
o_{p}(N^{1/40})
.

Then with
n:=\sum_{i=1}^{M}n_{i}
,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(n\tilde{H}_{n}-\mathbb{E}[n\tilde{H}_{n}]\right)\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2}),

(B.4)

where

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle:=(g_{1}+g_{3})\int{\rm Var}[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z]\phi(z){\rm d}z

\displaystyle+(3-2g_{1}-2g_{3}+g_{2})\int{\rm Cov}(K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}),K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z)\phi(z){\rm d}z

\displaystyle-4\int{\rm Cov}(K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}),g(\tilde{\Delta})|\tilde{Z}=z)\phi(z){\rm d}z+\mathbb{E}\left[{\rm Var}(g(\tilde{\Delta})|\tilde{Z})\right],

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\int\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}K(i,j)\frac{\pi_{i}f_{i}(z)}{\phi(z)}\frac{\pi_{j}f_{j}(z)}{\phi(z)}-\sum_{i=1}^{M}g(i)\frac{\pi_{i}f_{i}(z)}{\phi(z)}\right)^{2}\phi(z){\rm d}z

\displaystyle=\int\left(\mathbb{E}[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z]-\mathbb{E}[g(\tilde{\Delta})|\tilde{Z}=z]\right)^{2}\phi(z){\rm d}z.

Note that in the above displays,
\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}
are drawn independently from the conditional distribution
\tilde{\Delta}|\tilde{Z}=z
(recall the definition of
\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{Z}
in Section
2.1
);
g(\Delta):=2\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}K(\Delta,i)-\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}K(i,j)
, and
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}
are as defined in Theorem
4
.

Observe that
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
are not distribution-free in general, unless
\tilde{\Delta}
is independent of
\tilde{Z}
, i.e.,
{\rm H}_{0}
holds.
The proof of the above result is given in Appendix
C.12
, applying similar techniques as in
Bhattacharya 2020
.
Note that
Bhattacharya 2020
has applied the results in
Penrose 2007
, but some of the assumptions in
Penrose 2007

such as almost everywhere continuity and boundedness of densities are not explicitly stated in
Bhattacharya 2020
.
Here, due to the presence of a general kernel and the U-statistic term
\sum_{i\neq j}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})
we cannot directly apply the results in
Penrose 2007
, and hence we provide a complete proof under the assumptions stated above.

Remark B.1

(Non-degeneracy with characteristic kernel)
.

Suppose
K
is characteristic.
When
\tilde{\Delta}
is independent of
\tilde{Z}
, then
\kappa_{2}^{2}=0
, and
\kappa_{1}^{2}
reduces to the distribution-free null variance in Theorem
4
, which is strictly positive. When
\tilde{\Delta}
is not independent of
\tilde{Z}
,
\kappa_{2}^{2}
is strictly positive since the integrand is the fourth power of
the maximum mean discrepancy
9
9

9

MMD is a distance (provided that the kernel is characteristic) on the space of probability measures defined through the kernel
K(\cdot,\cdot)

[
33
]
. For two probabilities
P
and
Q
, let independent samples
\Delta_{1}^{P},\Delta_{2}^{P}
i.i.d. follow
P
, and
\Delta_{1}^{Q},\Delta_{2}^{Q}
i.i.d. follow
Q
. A definition of
MMD between
P,Q
is given by:

{\rm MMD}^{2}(P,Q):=\mathbb{E}K(\Delta_{1}^{P},\Delta_{2}^{P})+\mathbb{E}K(\Delta_{1}^{Q},\Delta_{2}^{Q})-2\mathbb{E}K(\Delta_{1}^{P},\Delta_{1}^{Q}).

(MMD) between the conditional distribution
\tilde{\Delta}|\tilde{Z}=z
and the unconditional distribution of
\tilde{\Delta}

(note that with a characteristic kernel, the MMD is positive whenever the two distributions are not equal).
Hence the asymptotic variance in (
B.4
) is always positive.

B.2
A CLT under Shrinking Alternatives

In the previous subsection, a CLT was derived under a fixed alternative.
In order to study the detection threshold of our statistic
\hat{\eta}
, a CLT under shrinking alternatives is needed, which will be studied in this subsection. Note that
Bhattacharya 2020
only provided a CLT under a fixed alternative (cf., Theorem
B.1
); the CLT under shrinking alternatives was not explicitly formulated, but was assumed to hold instead. As will be stated in the following theorem, it turns out that a uniform version of the conditions for the CLT under fixed alternatives (Theorem
B.1
) can guarantee a CLT under shrinking alternatives.

We assume for each
N
, the
M
distributions have densities
f_{1}^{N},\ldots,f_{M}^{N}
on
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
respectively.
We write a
N
at the top right of each
f_{i}
to emphasize that the densities change with the sample size
N
.

Assume
f_{i}^{N}(z)\overset{}{\longrightarrow}f(z)
as
N\to\infty
for a.e.
z\in\mathcal{Z}
(under the Lebesgue measure), for
i=1,\ldots,M
.
Let

\phi^{N}_{N}(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{N_{i}}{N}f_{i}^{N}(z)\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\phi^{N}(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}f_{i}^{N}(z),\quad{\rm for}\;\;z\in\mathcal{Z}.

Then
\phi^{N}_{N}(z)
is the marginal density of
Z
given the total number of observations.
To extend the CLT result to shrinking alternatives, we need to also extend the notion of stabilization for the non-homogeneous Poisson process
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi^{N}_{N}}
below. We note that the distribution of the set of points
\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}\}
is exactly a non-homogeneous Poisson process
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi^{N}_{N}}

[
53
, Proposition 1.5]
.

Consider the notation introduced at the start of Appendix
B
and recall the Poissonized setting.
Let
A^{N}
be a set with
\phi^{N}
-probability 1. Fix
x\in\mathcal{Z}
.

A radius of stabilization

R\equiv R(N,x)
at
x
exists if the points in
A^{N}\backslash B(x,N^{-1/d}R)
cannot impact the neighborhood of
x
in
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi^{N}_{N}}^{x}:=\mathcal{P}_{N\phi^{N}_{N}}\cup\{x\}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{E}\left(x,\mathcal{G}\left([\mathcal{P}_{N\phi^{N}_{N}}^{x}\cap B(x,N^{-1/d}R)]\cup\mathcal{X}\right)\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(x,\mathcal{G}\left(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi^{N}_{N}}\cap B(x,N^{-1/d}R)\right)\right),

(B.5)

for all finite
\mathcal{X}\subset A^{N}\backslash B(x,N^{-1/d}R)
.
With

\tau(t):=\sup_{N\geq 1,x\in A^{N}}\mathbb{P}[R(N,x)>t],\qquad\mbox{for }\;\;t>0,

\mathcal{G}
is said to be:

1.

power-law stabilizing
of order
q
with respect to
\phi^{N}_{N}
if
\sup_{t\geq 1}t^{q}\tau(t)<\infty
,

2.

exponentially stabilizing
with respect to
\phi^{N}_{N}
if
\limsup_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\log\tau(t)<0
.

Almost the same proof of Proposition
B.1
shows that if
\phi^{N}_{N}
has convex support and
\phi^{N}_{N}\geq c
on
{\rm supp}(\phi^{N}_{N})
for some
c>0
and all
N
, then
k
-NN graph is exponentially stabilizing with respect to
\phi^{N}_{N}
, and thus power-law stabilizing of any order
q>0
. The following theorem (proved in Appendix
C.13
) formally states the CLT under shrinking alternatives.

Theorem B.2

(CLT under shrinking alternatives)
.

Assume the Poissonized setting where we have
n_{i}\sim{\rm Poisson}(N_{i})
samples from the
i
-th distribution, for
i=1,\ldots,M
. Suppose the following assumptions hold:

1.

\sqrt{N}\left(\frac{N_{i}}{N}-\pi_{i}\right)\to 0
, as
N\to\infty
,
i=1,\ldots,M
, where
N=\sum_{i=1}^{M}N_{i}
.

2.

The
i
-th distribution has a Lebesgue density
f_{i}^{N}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
f_{i}^{N}
converges pointwise Lebesgue a.e. to a density
f
as
N\to\infty
, and
f_{i}^{N}
is equicontinuous almost everywhere, i.e., for Lebesgue a.e.
x
, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
|f_{i}^{N}(y)-f_{i}^{N}(x)|<\varepsilon
whenever
|y-x|<\delta
, for
i=1,\ldots,M
and all
N\geq 1
.

3.

f_{i}^{N}
is uniformly bounded above and has uniformly bounded support, i.e., there exists
C>0
such that
\|f_{i}^{N}\|_{\infty}<C
for all
N
and
i=1,\ldots,M
, and there exists a bounded set that contains
{\rm supp}(f_{i}^{N})
for all
N
and
i=1,\ldots,M
.

4.

\mathcal{G}
is translation and scale invariant, stabilizing on
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}
for some
\lambda>0
.

5.

\mathcal{G}
is power-law stabilizing with respect to
\phi_{N}^{N}
with order
q_{0}>d
, with the corresponding full probability sets
A^{N}
and radius of stabilization
R
as defined in (
B.5
).

6.

The degree bound (including in- and out-degrees) for
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}^{N}})
is
o_{p}(N^{1/40})
.

Then with
n=\sum_{i=1}^{M}n_{i}
,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(n\tilde{H}_{n}-\mathbb{E}[n\tilde{H}_{n}]\right)\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{1,{\rm null}}^{2}),

where

\kappa_{1,{\rm null}}^{2}:={a}\left({g}_{1}+{g}_{3}\right)+{b}\left({g}_{2}-2{g}_{1}-2{g}_{3}-1\right)+{c}\left({g}_{1}-{g}_{2}+{g}_{3}+1\right)>0

(B.6)

is equal to the numerator of the asymptotic null variance given in (
8
), which is distribution-free, not depending on
\{f_{i}^{N}\}_{i=1}^{M}
and
f
.

Remark B.2

(Conditions 2 and 3 for parametric models)
.

Suppose that
f_{i}^{N}(\cdot)=f(\cdot|\theta_{i}^{N})
is parametrized by
\theta_{i}^{N}
and the parametric family
\{f(\cdot|\theta):\theta\in\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}\}
(
p\geq 1
) with a common compact support
A
. If
\theta_{i}^{N}
depends on
N
and converges to
\theta_{0}
in the interior of
\Theta
as
N\to\infty
,
i=1,\ldots,M
, and
f(x|\theta)
is continuous in
(x,\theta)
, then conditions 2 and 3 above hold, because
f(x|\theta)
is uniformly continuous in
(x,\theta)\in A\times B(\theta_{0},\varepsilon)
(for some
\varepsilon>0
), a compact set.

B.3
Asymptotic Power and Detection Threshold

In this subsection, we consider
M=2
and study the power of the test based on
\hat{\eta}
along a parametric sub-model
[
11
]
:
suppose
P_{i}
has Lebesgue density
f(\cdot|\theta_{i})
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
belonging to the parametric family
\{f(\cdot|\theta):\theta\in\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}\}
(
p\geq 1
), for
i=1,2
.

Our analysis depends on Theorem
B.2
, the CLT under shrinking alternatives.
Under the null,
\mathbb{E}[n\tilde{H}_{n}]=0
.
Hence, the local power of the test that rejects the null when

\frac{\sqrt{n}\tilde{H}_{n}}{\kappa_{1,{\rm null}}}\geq z_{1-\alpha}\qquad{\rm or\ equivalently\ (asymptotically})\qquad\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\eta}}{\sigma_{\mathcal{G},K,d,\pi}}\geq z_{1-\alpha}

(B.7)

is determined by the rate at which
\mathbb{E}[n\tilde{H}_{n}]/\sqrt{n}
converges to 0, since

\frac{n\tilde{H}_{n}-\mathbb{E}[n\tilde{H}_{n}]}{\sqrt{n}\kappa_{1,{\rm null}}}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,1)

(recall that
\sigma_{\mathcal{G},K,d,\pi}
has been defined in (
8
), and
\kappa_{1,{\rm null}}
is defined in (
B.6
)). We next state the detection threshold of the test (
B.7
), which is of the same form as in
Bhattacharya 2020
.

Theorem B.3

(Detection threshold)
.

Suppose
\{\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
is a parametric family of distributions with an open convex parameter space
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
(for
p\geq 1
) and Lebesgue densities
\{f(\cdot|\theta)\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
. Assume:

1.

For all
\theta\in\Theta
,
f(\cdot|\theta)
has a compact and convex support
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with a nonempty interior, not depending on
\theta
.

2.

The conditions in Theorem
B.2
(that guarantee the CLT under shrinking alternatives) hold.

3.

For all
\theta\in\Theta
,
f(\cdot|\theta)
and
\nabla_{\theta}f(\cdot|\theta)
are three times continuously differentiable in the interior of
S
, and the Fisher information matrix

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim f(\cdot|\theta)}\left[\left(\frac{\nabla_{\theta}f(X|\theta)}{f(X|\theta)}\right)\left(\frac{\nabla_{\theta}f(X|\theta)}{f(X|\theta)}\right)^{\top}\right]
is positive definite for all
\theta\in\Theta
.

4.

For all
x\in S
,
f(x|\cdot)
is three times continuously differentiable in
\Theta
.

Suppose that
M=2
and the discrete kernel
K
and the
k
-NN graph (for a fixed
k\geq 1
) are used in defining
\hat{\eta}
. Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
have densities
f(\cdot|\theta_{1})
and
f(\cdot|\theta_{2})
respectively, and
\theta_{2}=\theta_{1}+\varepsilon_{N}
, where
\varepsilon_{N}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
. Let
\kappa_{1,{\rm null}}
be as defined in Theorem
B.2
, and
{\rm H}_{x}f(x|\theta_{1})
be the Hessian of
f(x|\theta_{1})
taken w.r.t.
x
. Also, define, for
h\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

a_{k,\theta_{1}}(h):=-\frac{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}(1-2\pi_{2})C_{k,2}}{2kd\kappa_{1,{\rm null}}}\int_{S}h^{\top}\nabla_{\theta_{1}}\left(\frac{{\rm tr}({\rm H}_{x}f(x|\theta_{1}))}{f(x|\theta_{1})}\right)f^{\frac{d-2}{d}}(x|\theta_{1}){\rm d}x,

(B.8)

b_{k,\theta_{1}}(h):=\frac{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}}{\sigma_{\mathcal{G},K,d,\pi}}\mathbb{E}_{X\sim f(\cdot|\theta_{1})}\Bigg[\frac{h^{\top}\nabla_{\theta_{1}}f(X|\theta_{1})}{f(X|\theta_{1})}\Bigg]^{2},\ {\rm with}\ C_{k,2}:=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}:(\mathbf{0},x)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{k{\rm NN}}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\|x\|^{2}\Bigg].

1.

If the dimension
d\leq 8
, then the following hold:

(a)

\|N^{\frac{1}{4}}\varepsilon_{N}\|\to 0
: The limiting power of the test (
B.7
) is
\alpha
.

(b)

N^{\frac{1}{4}}\varepsilon_{N}\to h
:

If
d\leq 7
, limiting power of the test (
B.7
) is

\Phi\left(z_{\alpha}+b_{k,\theta_{1}}(h)\right)
.

If
d=8
and the limiting power is

\Phi\left(z_{\alpha}+a_{k,\theta_{1}}(h)+b_{k,\theta_{1}}(h)\right)
.

(c)

\|N^{\frac{1}{4}}\varepsilon_{N}\|\to\infty
: The limiting power of the test (
B.7
) is 1.

2.

If the dimension
d\geq 9
, then the following hold:

(a)

\|N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{d}}\varepsilon_{N}\|\to 0
: The limiting power of the test (
B.7
) is
\alpha
.

(b)

N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{d}}\varepsilon_{N}\to h
: The limiting power of the test (
B.7
) is

\Phi\left(z_{\alpha}+a_{k,\theta_{1}}(h)\right)
.

(c)

\|N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{d}}\varepsilon_{N}\|\to\infty
such that
\|N^{\frac{2}{d}}\varepsilon_{N}\|\to 0
: Then depending on whether

N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{d}}(1-2\pi_{2})\int_{S}\varepsilon_{N}^{\top}\nabla_{\theta_{1}}\left(\frac{{\rm tr}({\rm H}_{x}f(x|\theta_{1}))}{f(x|\theta_{1})}\right)f^{\frac{d-2}{d}}(x|\theta_{1}){\rm d}x\to\left\{\begin{aligned} &\infty,\\
&-\infty,\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(B.9)

the limiting power of the test (
B.7
) is 0 or 1, respectively.

(d)

N^{\frac{2}{d}}\varepsilon_{N}\to h
: The limiting power of the test (
B.7
) is 0 or 1, depending on whether
a_{k,\theta_{1}}(h)+b_{k,\theta_{1}}(h)
is negative or positive, respectively.

(e)

N^{\frac{2}{d}}\varepsilon_{N}\to\infty
: The limiting power of the test (
B.7
) is 1.

A proof of the above result is given in Appendix
C.14
.
In particular,
the above result shows that the detection threshold of
\hat{\eta}
exhibits a “
d=8
” phenomenon (see
Bhattacharya 2020
):
When
d\leq 8
, the detection threshold is
N^{-\frac{1}{4}}
;
while when
d\geq 9
, the detection threshold is somewhere between
N^{-\frac{2}{d}}
and
N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{d}}
,
depending on the direction of
\varepsilon_{N}
and the sign of
a_{k,\theta_{1}}
; see (
B.8
) and (
B.9
).
If
\varepsilon_{N}=\delta_{N}h
for
\delta_{N}>0
and some fixed
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and

a_{k,\theta_{1}}(h)>0
, then the detection threshold is
\delta_{N}\sim N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{d}}
;
on the other hand, if
a_{k,\theta_{1}}(h)<0
, then the detection threshold is
\delta_{N}\sim N^{-\frac{2}{d}}
.
When
a_{k,\theta_{1}}(h)=0
, the precise location of the detection threshold has to be determined on a case by case basis (see
[
11
]
).
In Appendix
D.2
, we empirically show that the same detection threshold also holds for the non-Poissonized setting, i.e., the original setting in Section
2
of our paper where the
n_{i}
’s are nonrandom constants (instead of
n_{i}\sim{\rm Poisson}(N_{i})
).

Appendix C
Proofs of the Main Results

C.1
Proof of Lemma
1

If
P_{1}=\ldots=P_{M}=:P
, then
\tilde{Z}
follows
P
regardless of
\Delta
, and so
\tilde{\Delta}\perp\!\!\!\perp\tilde{Z}
.
Conversely, if
\tilde{\Delta}\perp\!\!\!\perp\tilde{Z}
, then the conditional distribution
\tilde{Z}|(\tilde{\Delta}=i)
should be the same for all
i=1,\ldots,M
, which implies
P_{1}=\ldots=P_{M}
. Hence the
M
distributions are the same if and only if
\tilde{\Delta}\perp\!\!\!\perp\tilde{Z}
.

If there exist disjoint measurable sets
\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{M}
such that
P_{i}(A_{i})=1
,
then we can almost surely determine the label of
\tilde{Z}
, by finding the
A_{i}
that
\tilde{Z}
belongs to, i.e.,
\tilde{\Delta}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}iI(\tilde{Z}\in A_{i})
can be written as a measurable function of
\tilde{Z}
.
Conversely, if
\tilde{\Delta}
is a measurable function of
\tilde{Z}
,
then the
\mathcal{E}(\tilde{Z})
-measurable
10
10

10

\mathcal{E}(\tilde{Z})
is the smallest
\sigma
-algebra such that
\tilde{Z}
is measurable.
set
A_{i}
defined as
\tilde{\Delta}^{-1}(i)
satisfies
P_{i}(A_{i})=\mathbb{P}(\tilde{Z}\in A_{i}|\tilde{\Delta}=i)=1
.
Hence there exist disjoint measurable sets
\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{M}
such that
P_{i}(A_{i})=1
if and only
\tilde{\Delta}
is a measurable function of
\tilde{Z}
. ∎

C.2
Proof of Theorem
1

Note that
\eta
is the kernel measure of association between
\tilde{Z}
and
\tilde{\Delta}
proposed in
[
21
, Theorem 2.1]
. Given the kernel
K(\cdot,\cdot)
is characteristic, it satisfies:

(i)

\eta
is a deterministic number between
[0,1]
;

(ii)

\eta=0
if and only if
\tilde{\Delta}\perp\!\!\!\perp\tilde{Z}
, and

(iii)

\eta=1
if and only if
\tilde{\Delta}
is a measurable function of
\tilde{Z}
.

Hence the results follow from Lemma
1
.
∎

C.3
Proof of Proposition
2

Define
(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}},\tilde{Z},\tilde{Z}^{\prime})
as follows:

1.

Draw
(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime},\tilde{Z})
according to the distribution mentioned in Section
2.1
(see (
2
)), i.e.,

(a)

Draw
\tilde{\Delta}\sim\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\delta_{i}
, where
\delta_{i}
is the Dirac measure at
i
.

(b)

Draw
\tilde{Z}\mid(\tilde{\Delta}=i)\sim P_{i}
.

(c)

Draw
\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}
independently from the conditional distribution of
\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}
, given the value of
\tilde{Z}
from (b).

2.

Draw
\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
from the distribution
\kappa(\tilde{Z},\cdot)
.

Then
\tilde{Z}^{\prime}\sim\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}Q_{i}
, and
\eta(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{M})
can be defined from
(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}},\tilde{Z}^{\prime})
, as
\eta(P_{1},\ldots,P_{M})
can be defined from
(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}},\tilde{Z})
.

The denominator and the second term of the numerator of
\eta
in (
2
) are unchanged when
P_{1},\ldots,P_{M}
are transitioned to
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{M}
as these only depend on the mixture proportion
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{M}
which is unchanged.
Hence it suffices to consider the first term in the numerator and show:

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}})|\tilde{Z}\big]\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}})|\tilde{Z}^{\prime}\big]\right],

(C.1)

where on the left-hand side,
\tilde{\Delta}
and
\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}}
are i.i.d. drawn from the conditional distribution of
\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}
,
and on the right-hand side,
\tilde{\Delta}
and
\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}}
are i.i.d. drawn from the conditional distribution of
\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
.

We first consider the case where
\tilde{\Delta}
is not a deterministic function of
\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
.
Consider the kernel partial correlation
[
42
]
between
\tilde{\Delta}
and
\tilde{Z}
given
\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
, defined by

\rho^{2}(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{Z}|\tilde{Z}^{\prime})=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}})|(\tilde{Z},\tilde{Z}^{\prime})\big]\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\big[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}})|\tilde{Z}^{\prime}\big]\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\big[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}})|\tilde{Z}^{\prime}\big]\right]},

where in
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}})|(\tilde{Z},\tilde{Z}^{\prime})\big]\right]
,
\tilde{\Delta}
and
\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}}
are i.i.d. drawn from the conditional distribution of
\tilde{\Delta}\mid(\tilde{Z},\tilde{Z}^{\prime})
and then averaged over
(\tilde{Z},\tilde{Z}^{\prime})
.
Since
\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
is drawn from
\kappa(\tilde{Z},\cdot)
,

\mathbb{E}\big[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}})|(\tilde{Z},\tilde{Z}^{\prime})\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}})|\tilde{Z}\big]
, and therefore

\rho^{2}(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{Z}|\tilde{Z}^{\prime})=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}})|\tilde{Z}\big]\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\big[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}})|\tilde{Z}^{\prime}\big]\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\big[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}{{}^{\prime}})|\tilde{Z}^{\prime}\big]\right]}.

It is shown in
Huang
et al. 2022
that the
denominator of
\rho^{2}(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{Z}|\tilde{Z}^{\prime})
is positive, and the
numerator of
\rho^{2}(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{Z}|\tilde{Z}^{\prime})
is nonnegative.
Hence inequality (
C.1
) holds.
By
Huang
et al. 2022
,
\rho^{2}(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{Z}|\tilde{Z}^{\prime})=0
if and only if
\tilde{\Delta}
is conditionally independent of
\tilde{Z}
given
\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
, which is the same as saying
\tilde{\Delta}\mid(\tilde{Z},\tilde{Z}^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
.
Since
\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
is obtained by passing
\tilde{Z}
through
\kappa
, this is further equivalent to
\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
.

If
\tilde{\Delta}
is a deterministic function of
\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
, then
\tilde{\Delta}
must also be a deterministic function of
\tilde{Z}
, and the inequality (
C.1
) holds with equality, and
\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
holds as well.

In the case of a deterministic data processing, a function
T:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is applied to all the data points from the
M
distributions.
In such a case,
\kappa(z,\cdot)=\delta_{T(z)}
, the Dirac measure at
T(z)
.
If
T
is a bijection, then
\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{\Delta}\mid T(\tilde{Z})=\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
, so the equality in (
C.1
) is attained, showing that a bijective transformation of the
M
distributions does not change our measure of dissimilarity
\eta
. ∎

C.4
Proof of Proposition
3

From (
3
), it suffices to show the convexity for the first term in the numerator.
Let
\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{M}(P_{i}+Q_{i})
be a dominating measure. The first term in the numerator of (
3
)
can be re-written as:

\int\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}K(i,j)\frac{\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{i}P_{i})}{{\rm d}\mu}}{\frac{{\rm d}\bar{P}}{{\rm d}\mu}}\frac{\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{j}P_{j})}{{\rm d}\mu}}{\frac{{\rm d}\bar{P}}{{\rm d}\mu}}\frac{{\rm d}\bar{P}}{{\rm d}\mu}{\rm d}\mu(z)=\int\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}K(i,j)\frac{\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{i}P_{i})}{{\rm d}\mu}\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{j}P_{j})}{{\rm d}\mu}}{\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{1}P_{1})}{{\rm d}\mu}+\ldots+\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{M}P_{M})}{{\rm d}\mu}}{\rm d}\mu(z).

Note that
f:\mathbb{R}^{M}\to\mathbb{R}
,

x\mapsto\frac{x^{\top}[K(i,j)]_{i,j=1}^{M}x}{1^{\top}x}
is a convex function on
1^{\top}x>0

[
15
, Exercise 3.23]
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\int\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}K(i,j)\frac{\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{i}(\lambda P_{i}+(1-\lambda)Q_{i}))}{{\rm d}\mu}\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{j}(\lambda P_{j}+(1-\lambda)Q_{j}))}{{\rm d}\mu}}{\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{1}(\lambda P_{1}+(1-\lambda)Q_{1}))}{{\rm d}\mu}+\ldots+\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{M}(\lambda P_{M}+(1-\lambda)Q_{M}))}{{\rm d}\mu}}{\rm d}\mu(z)

\displaystyle=\int f\left(\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{1}(\lambda P_{1}+(1-\lambda)Q_{1}))}{{\rm d}\mu}(z),\ldots,\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{M}(\lambda P_{M}+(1-\lambda)Q_{M}))}{{\rm d}\mu}(z)\right){\rm d}\mu(z)

\displaystyle\leq\int\left[\lambda f\left(\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{1}P_{1})}{{\rm d}\mu},\ldots,\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{M}P_{M})}{{\rm d}\mu}\right)+(1-\lambda)f\left(\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{1}Q_{1})}{{\rm d}\mu},\ldots,\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{M}Q_{M})}{{\rm d}\mu}\right)\right]{\rm d}\mu(z)

\displaystyle=\lambda\int\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}K(i,j)\frac{\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{i}P_{i})}{{\rm d}\mu}\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{j}P_{j})}{{\rm d}\mu}}{\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{1}P_{1})}{{\rm d}\mu}+\ldots+\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{M}P_{M})}{{\rm d}\mu}}{\rm d}\mu(z)

\displaystyle+(1-\lambda)\int\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}K(i,j)\frac{\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{i}Q_{i})}{{\rm d}\mu}\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{j}Q_{j})}{{\rm d}\mu}}{\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{1}Q_{1})}{{\rm d}\mu}+\ldots+\frac{{\rm d}(\pi_{M}Q_{M})}{{\rm d}\mu}}{\rm d}\mu(z),

and the joint convexity of
\eta
follows. ∎

C.5
Proof of a Special Case of
\eta

Suppose
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
M=2
, and that
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
have densities
f
and
g
w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,

\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\Delta}=2|Z=z)=\frac{\pi_{2}g(z)}{\pi_{1}f(z)+\pi_{2}g(z)}.

Further,
\eta
can be written as
\eta=1-\frac{\mathbb{E}[\rho(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime})]}{\mathbb{E}[\rho(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})]}
(see
[
21
, Equation 2.1]
), where
\rho(s,s^{\prime}):=\|K(s,\cdot)-K(s^{\prime},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
,
11
11

11

\mathcal{H}
is the RKHS induced by kernel
K
on
{\mathcal{S}}:=\{1,\ldots,M\}
equipped with inner product
\langle K(s,\cdot),K(s^{\prime},\cdot)\rangle=K(s,s^{\prime})
, for
s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
.
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\rho(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}[\rho(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime})|Z]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left\{2\rho(1,2)\frac{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}f(x)g(x)}{[\pi_{1}f(x)+\pi_{2}g(x)]^{2}}\right\}[\pi_{1}f(x)+\pi_{2}g(x)]\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\rho(1,2)\int\frac{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}f(x)g(x)}{\pi_{1}f(x)+\pi_{2}g(x)}\mathrm{d}x,

and

\mathbb{E}\big[\rho(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})\big]=2\rho(1,2)\,\pi_{1}\pi_{2}.

Therefore,

\eta=1-\int\frac{f(x)g(x)}{\pi_{1}f(x)+\pi_{2}g(x)}\mathrm{d}x.

This completes the argument. ∎

C.6
Proof of Proposition
1

We will use the expression in Appendix
C.5
of
\eta
, i.e.,
\eta=1-\int\frac{f(x)g(x)}{\pi_{1}f(x)+\pi_{2}g(x)}\mathrm{d}x.
. First consider the location family. Here, we have:

\eta=1-\int\frac{1}{\pi_{1}\frac{1}{f(x)}+\pi_{2}\frac{1}{f(x-\theta)}}\mathrm{d}x.

Since
f(x)
is log-concave,
\frac{1}{f(x)}
is convex as
\exp(\cdot)
is convex and increasing.
Write
g:=\frac{1}{f}
. Then

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=1-\int\frac{1}{\pi_{1}g(x)+\pi_{2}g(x-\theta)}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=1-\int\frac{1}{\pi_{1}g(x+\pi_{2}\theta)+\pi_{2}g(x-\pi_{1}\theta)}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=1-\int\frac{1}{\pi_{1}g(x+\pi_{2}\lambda h)+\pi_{2}g(x-\pi_{1}\lambda h)}\mathrm{d}x.

It suffices to show that
\pi_{1}g(x+\pi_{2}\lambda h)+\pi_{2}g(x-\pi_{1}\lambda h)
is increasing in
\lambda
, i.e.,
for
0\leq\lambda\leq\Lambda
,

\pi_{1}g(x+\pi_{2}\lambda h)+\pi_{2}g(x-\pi_{1}\lambda h)\leq\pi_{1}g(x+\pi_{2}\Lambda h)+\pi_{2}g(x-\pi_{1}\Lambda h).

(C.2)

Write
x-\pi_{1}\lambda h
and
x+\pi_{2}\lambda h
as convex combinations of
x-\pi_{1}\Lambda h
and
x+\pi_{2}\Lambda h
, i.e.,

\displaystyle x-\pi_{1}\lambda h

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{1}(\Lambda-\lambda)}{\Lambda}(x+\pi_{2}\Lambda h)+\frac{\pi_{1}\lambda+\pi_{2}\Lambda}{\Lambda}(x-\pi_{1}\Lambda h),

\displaystyle x+\pi_{2}\lambda h

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{1}\Lambda+\pi_{2}\lambda}{\Lambda}(x+\pi_{2}\Lambda h)+\frac{\pi_{2}(\Lambda-\lambda)}{\Lambda}(x-\pi_{1}\Lambda h).

By the convexity of
g
, we have:

\displaystyle\pi_{1}g(x+\pi_{2}\lambda h)+\pi_{2}g(x-\pi_{1}\lambda h)

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{1}\left(\frac{\pi_{1}\Lambda+\pi_{2}\lambda}{\Lambda}g(x+\pi_{2}\Lambda h)+\frac{\pi_{2}(\Lambda-\lambda)}{\Lambda}g(x-\pi_{1}\Lambda h)\right)

\displaystyle+\pi_{2}\left(\frac{\pi_{1}(\Lambda-\lambda)}{\Lambda}g(x+\pi_{2}\Lambda h)+\frac{\pi_{1}\lambda+\pi_{2}\Lambda}{\Lambda}g(x-\pi_{1}\Lambda h)\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}g(x+\pi_{2}\Lambda h)+\pi_{2}g(x-\pi_{1}\Lambda h).

Hence the monotonicity is proved.
When
\lambda=0
,
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are equal, so
\eta=0
. Since
f(\cdot)
is a density,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-\lambda h)\mathrm{d}\lambda<+\infty
for Lebesgue almost every
x
,
which implies the convex function
\pi_{2}g(x-\lambda h)=\pi_{2}\frac{1}{f(x-\lambda h)}\to+\infty
as
\lambda\to+\infty

for Lebesgue almost every
x
. Hence, by dominated convergence,

\eta=1-\int\frac{1}{\pi_{1}g(x)+\pi_{2}g(x-\lambda h)}\mathrm{d}x\to 1\quad\mathrm{as}\quad\lambda\to+\infty.

To show that the monotonicity is strict, we can first suppose that
f(x)=0
on the boundary of its support since the boundary of a convex set has 0 Lebesgue measure, so it will not change the value of
\eta
.
Since
\eta<1
,
\pi_{1}g(x+\pi_{2}\lambda h)+\pi_{2}g(x-\pi_{1}\lambda h)
is not
+\infty
for Lebesgue almost every
x
.
Hence we can find an
x
such that the left-hand side of (
C.2
) is finite.
We want to further find an
x
such that the inequality in (
C.2
) is strict.
Note that if equality holds in (
C.2
), then by the convexity,
g(\cdot)
must be a linear function on the line segment
[x-\pi_{1}\Lambda h,x+\pi_{2}\Lambda h]
.
Since
g
is log-convex, it must be a constant on
[x-\pi_{1}\Lambda h,x+\pi_{2}\Lambda h]
.
Note that
\lim_{x\to\infty}g(x)=\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{1}{f(x)}=\infty
.
Hence we can move
x
along the line passing through
x-\pi_{1}\Lambda h
and
x+\pi_{2}\Lambda h
until inequality (
C.2
) becomes strict (and the left-hand side is still finite).
Now, since a convex function is continuous in the interior of its domain, (
C.2
) with strict inequality can still hold in a neighborhood of
x
, so that after integration we obtain
\eta(P_{1},P_{2}(\lambda))<\eta(P_{1},P_{2}(\Lambda))
.

Now consider the case of a scale family. Note that

\eta=1-\int\frac{1}{\pi_{1}\frac{1}{f(x)}+\pi_{2}\frac{1}{\lambda f(\lambda x)}}\mathrm{d}x.

By a change of variable
y=\lambda^{\pi_{2}}x
we get

\eta=1-\int\frac{1}{\pi_{1}\lambda^{\pi_{2}}g\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)+\pi_{2}\frac{1}{\lambda^{\pi_{1}}}g(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y)}\mathrm{d}y,

where
g(x)=\frac{1}{f(x)}
is still a convex function as before.

We will show that
\phi(\lambda):=\pi_{1}\lambda^{\pi_{2}}g\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)+\pi_{2}\frac{1}{\lambda^{\pi_{1}}}g(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y)

is decreasing on
(0,1]
, and increasing on
[1,\infty)
. We can suppose both
\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}
and
\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y
lie in the interior of
{\rm supp}(f)
,
because if any of them is not in the interior of
{\rm supp}(f)
, then if
\lambda\geq 1
,
\phi(\Lambda)=+\infty
for any
\Lambda>\lambda
by the convexity of
g
, and similarly
if
\lambda<1
, then
\phi(\delta)=+\infty
for any
0<\delta<\lambda
. Given that both the points are in the interior of
{\rm supp}(f)
, we can take the derivative of
\phi
to obtain

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\left(\pi_{2}\lambda^{\pi_{2}-1}g\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)-\lambda^{\pi_{2}}\nabla g\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)^{\top}\frac{\pi_{2}y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}+1}}\right)

\displaystyle+\pi_{2}\left(-\frac{\pi_{1}}{\lambda^{\pi_{1}+1}}g(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y)+\frac{1}{\lambda^{\pi_{1}}}\nabla g(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y)^{\top}\pi_{1}\lambda^{\pi_{1}-1}y\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}}{\lambda}\left(\lambda^{\pi_{2}}g\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)-\nabla g\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)^{\top}y-\frac{1}{\lambda^{\pi_{1}}}g(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y)+\nabla g(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y)^{\top}y\right).

Note that
\phi^{\prime}(1)=0
. Let
\psi(\lambda):=\lambda^{\pi_{2}}g\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)-\nabla g\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)^{\top}y-\frac{1}{\lambda^{\pi_{1}}}g(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y)+\nabla g(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y)^{\top}y
. It suffices to show that
\psi^{\prime}(\lambda)>0
. Observe that

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\lambda^{\pi_{2}-1}g\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)-\lambda^{\pi_{2}}\nabla g\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)^{\top}\frac{\pi_{2}y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}+1}}+y^{\top}\nabla^{2}g\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)y\cdot\frac{\pi_{2}}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\pi_{1}}{\lambda^{\pi_{1}+1}}g(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y)-\frac{1}{\lambda^{\pi_{1}}}\nabla g(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y)^{\top}\pi_{1}\lambda^{\pi_{1}-1}y+\pi_{1}\lambda^{\pi_{1}-1}y^{\top}\nabla^{2}g(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y)y

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\lambda^{\pi_{2}-1}L\left(\frac{y}{\lambda^{\pi_{2}}}\right)+\frac{\pi_{1}}{\lambda^{\pi_{1}+1}}L\left(\lambda^{\pi_{1}}y\right),

where
L(x):=g(x)-\nabla g(x)^{\top}x+x^{\top}\nabla^{2}g(x)x
.
Since
f(x)
is log-concave,
g(x)=\frac{1}{f(x)}=\exp(h(x))
for some convex function
h(x)
. Hence

\displaystyle L(x)

\displaystyle=e^{h(x)}-e^{h(x)}\nabla h(x)^{\top}x+x^{\top}\left(e^{h(x)}\nabla h(x)\nabla h(x)^{\top}+e^{h(x)}\nabla^{2}h(x)\right)x

\displaystyle=e^{h(x)}\left(1-\nabla h(x)^{\top}x+(\nabla h(x)^{\top}x)^{2}\right)+e^{h(x)}x^{\top}\nabla^{2}h(x)x

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4}e^{h(x)}+e^{h(x)}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\nabla h(x)^{\top}x\right)^{2}+e^{h(x)}x^{\top}\nabla^{2}h(x)x>0.

Hence
\psi^{\prime}(\lambda)>0
and the monotonicity is proved.

When
\lambda=1
,
P_{1}=P_{2}
, and so
\eta=0
.
As
\lambda\to 0^{+}
, no matter
f(0)=0
or
f(0)>0
, we have
\pi_{1}\frac{1}{f(x)}+\pi_{2}\frac{1}{\lambda f(\lambda x)}\to+\infty
;
so dominated convergence implies

\eta=1-\int\frac{1}{\pi_{1}\frac{1}{f(x)}+\pi_{2}\frac{1}{\lambda f(\lambda x)}}\mathrm{d}x\to 1.

As
\lambda\to+\infty
, by a change of variable
y=\lambda x
and again using dominated convergence theorem, we get

\eta=1-\int\frac{1}{\pi_{1}\frac{\lambda}{f\left(\frac{y}{\lambda}\right)}+\pi_{2}\frac{1}{f(y)}}\mathrm{d}y\to 1.

This completes the proof of the result.
∎

C.7
Proof of Theorem
2

Here we provide a convenient proof of Theorem
2
using the consistency result in
Deb
et al. 2020
and a coupling of our data to a process where
\Delta_{i}
’s are i.i.d. Multinoulli(
\pi
) with
\pi=(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{M})
.

Suppose
X^{(m)}_{1},X^{(m)}_{2}\ldots\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}P_{m}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
.
Let
\tilde{\Delta}_{i}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}{\rm Multinoulli}(\pi)
.
If
\tilde{\Delta}_{i}=m
, we draw
\tilde{Z}_{i}
according to
P_{m}
. More specifically, for
i=1,\ldots,n
,

\tilde{Z}_{i}:=\sum_{m=1}^{M}I(\tilde{\Delta}_{i}=m)X^{(m)}_{\sum_{j=1}^{i}I(\tilde{\Delta}_{j}=m)}.

Let
\tilde{n}_{m}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}I(\tilde{\Delta}_{i}=m)
, and
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
be the graph constructed on the pooled sample

(\tilde{Z}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{n})
, which is

X^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(1)}_{\tilde{n}_{1}},X^{(2)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(2)}_{\tilde{n}_{2}},\ldots,X_{1}^{(M)},\ldots,X^{(M)}_{\tilde{n}_{M}}
,
after proper permutation.
Define:

\tilde{\eta}:=\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(\tilde{Z}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{j})\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{i},\tilde{\Delta}_{j})-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{i},\tilde{\Delta}_{j})}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{i},\tilde{\Delta}_{i})-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{i},\tilde{\Delta}_{j})},

where
\tilde{d}_{i}
is the out-degree of vertex
i
in the graph
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
.

From the proof of Theorem
1
we see that
\eta
is the kernel measure of association
[
21
]
between
\tilde{Z}
and
\tilde{\Delta}
. Here
\tilde{\eta}
is the empirical estimator of
\eta
proposed in
[
21
]
. We can check the conditions required for the consistency of
\tilde{\eta}
(i.e.,
\tilde{\eta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\eta
) as follows:
Note that any function
f\in\mathcal{H}
12
12

12

\mathcal{H}
is the RKHS induced by kernel
K
on
{\mathcal{S}}:=\{1,\ldots,M\}
equipped with inner product
\langle K(s,\cdot),K(s^{\prime},\cdot)\rangle=K(s,s^{\prime})
, for
s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
.
can be written as
f(s)=f(1)I(s=1)+\ldots+f(M)I(s=M)
, for
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
which implies
\mathcal{H}
is finite-dimensional and hence separable.
As any kernel on the finite set
{\mathcal{S}}
is bounded, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{Z}\sim Q}[K^{4+\varepsilon}(\tilde{Z},\tilde{Z})]<+\infty
for any probability distribution
Q
.
Now from
[
21
, Theorem 3.1]
it follows that
\tilde{\eta}\to\eta
almost surely.

Recall that
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
is the graph constructed on the pooled samples

(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})=\left(X^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(1)}_{{n}_{1}},X^{(2)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(2)}_{{n}_{2}},\ldots,X_{1}^{(M)},\ldots,X^{(M)}_{{n}_{M}}\right),

with
n_{1},\ldots,n_{M}
being nonrandom, and

\hat{\eta}=\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}K({\Delta}_{i},{\Delta}_{j})-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K({\Delta}_{i},{\Delta}_{j})}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K({\Delta}_{i},{\Delta}_{i})-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K({\Delta}_{i},{\Delta}_{j})},

with
(\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{n})=(1,\ldots,1,\ldots,M,\ldots,M)
being the class labels such that
n_{m}
of them is
m
, i.e.,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}I(\Delta_{i}=m)=n_{m}
, for
m=1,\ldots,M
.
The goal is to show that
\hat{\eta}-\tilde{\eta}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}0
.

By the strong law of large numbers,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{i},\tilde{\Delta}_{i})\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{1})]=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}K(i,i)=\lim_{\frac{n_{i}}{n}\to\pi_{i}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K({\Delta}_{i},{\Delta}_{i}).

By the strong law of large numbers for U-statistics, we have

\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{i},\tilde{\Delta}_{j})\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})]=\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}K(i,j)=\lim_{\frac{n_{i}}{n}\to\pi_{i}}\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K({\Delta}_{i},{\Delta}_{j}).

Since
K
is characteristic,
whenever
\tilde{\Delta}_{1}\neq\tilde{\Delta}_{2}
,

K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{1})+K(\tilde{\Delta}_{2},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})-2K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})=\|K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\cdot)-K(\tilde{\Delta}_{2},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}>0
.
This implies
\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{1})-\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})>0
, so the denominator of
\hat{\eta}
has a nonzero deterministic limit. Hence we only need to show

\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}K({\Delta}_{i},{\Delta}_{j})-\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(\tilde{Z}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{j})\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{i},\tilde{\Delta}_{j})\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}0.

Recall that
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
is the graph constructed on
X^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(1)}_{{n}_{1}},X^{(2)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(2)}_{{n}_{2}},\ldots,X_{1}^{(M)},\ldots,X^{(M)}_{{n}_{M}}
, and
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
is the graph constructed on

X^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(1)}_{\tilde{n}_{1}},X^{(2)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(2)}_{\tilde{n}_{2}},\ldots,X_{1}^{(M)},\ldots,X^{(M)}_{\tilde{n}_{M}}
.
Hence
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
can be obtained from
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
be replacing
\sum_{i=1}^{M}|n_{i}-\tilde{n}_{i}|/2
points.
Note that replacing one point changes at most
q_{n}+t_{n}
edges.
More specifically, if
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
can be obtained from
\mathcal{G}_{n}
by replacing one point with another point (possibly elsewhere), then

\#\{(j,k):(j,k)\in\left(\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\setminus\mathcal{E}(\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n})\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{E}(\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n})\setminus\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\right){\rm\ or\ }i\in\{j,k\}\}\leq q_{n}+t_{n}.

Hence:

\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(\tilde{Z}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{j})\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{i},\tilde{\Delta}_{j})\right|\leq\frac{2(q_{n}+t_{n})\|K\|_{\infty}}{nr_{n}}\leq\frac{C}{n},

for some
C>0
. Therefore, for general
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
, by replacing
\sum_{i=1}^{M}|n_{i}-\tilde{n}_{i}|/2
points,

\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(\tilde{Z}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{j})\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{i},\tilde{\Delta}_{j})\right|\leq\frac{C\sum_{i=1}^{M}|n_{i}-\tilde{n}_{i}|}{2n}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}0.

This completes the proof of the theorem. ∎

C.8
Proof of Theorem
3

We will use the following result (proved in Appendix
C.15
) which is an extension of
Pham
et al. 1989
.

Theorem C.1
.

Suppose we have real numbers
\{a_{ij}\}_{i,j=1}^{n}
,
\{b_{ij}\}_{i,j=1}^{n}
(
a_{ij}
,
b_{ij}
depend on
n
) satisfying
a_{ii}=b_{ii}=0
,
a_{ij}=a_{ji}
,
b_{ij}=b_{ji}
for all
i,j
, and
\sum a_{ij}=\sum b_{ij}=0
.
Let
(R_{1},\ldots,R_{n})
be a random permutation of
(1,\ldots,n)
, with all permutations being equally likely.
Set
B_{ij}:=b_{R_{i}R_{j}}
.
Suppose
t_{n}
is a deterministic sequence such that
\frac{t_{n}^{r}}{n}\to 0
, as
n\to\infty
, for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
, and

(A1)

\max_{i}\sum_{j}|a_{ij}|=O(\max|a_{ij}|\cdot t_{n})
,

(A2)

\liminf_{n}\sum a_{ij}^{2}/(n\max a_{ij}^{2})>0
,

(B1)

\limsup_{n}\left(\frac{2\sum b_{i+}^{2}}{n}\right)/\sum b_{ij}^{2}<1
,
where
b_{i+}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ij}
,

(B2)

\sum|b_{ij}|^{r}/n^{2}=O\left((\sum b_{ij}^{2}/n^{2})^{\frac{r}{2}}\right)
,    for
r=3,4,\ldots
.

Then

\frac{\sum a_{ij}B_{ij}}{w_{n}}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,1)

where
w_{n}^{2}:=4\sum^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{ik}\sum^{\prime}b_{ij}b_{ik}/n^{3}+2\sum a_{ij}^{2}\sum b_{ij}^{2}/n^{2}
, and
\sum^{\prime}
means summing over all possible distinct indices (so here it means summing over all distinct triplets
(i,j,k)
).

Remark C.1

(On the normalizing constant
w_{n}
)
.

Note that

w_{n}^{2}
is not the variance of
\sum a_{ij}B_{ij}
but they are asymptotically equivalent in the sense that

w_{n}^{2}/{\rm Var}(\sum a_{ij}B_{ij})\to 1
. We write
w_{n}
here because it arises naturally in the proof, and it can be replaced by
{\rm Var}(\sum a_{ij}B_{ij})
in the statement of Theorem
C.1
.

We now show how Theorem
C.1
can be used to prove Theorem
3
.
Note that given
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, the denominator of
\hat{\eta}
is deterministic, so we only need to focus on the numerator of
\hat{\eta}
. For
i\neq j
, let

\displaystyle a_{ij}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{d_{i}}I\{(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\}+\frac{1}{d_{j}}I\{(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\}-\frac{2}{n-1},

(C.3)

\displaystyle b_{ij}

\displaystyle=K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{p\neq q}K(\Delta_{p},\Delta_{q}).

Then,
a_{ii}=b_{ii}=0
for all
i
, and
\sum a_{ij}=\sum b_{ij}=0
. Note that
\sum a_{ij}B_{ij}
has the same distribution as the permutation distribution of (the numerator of)
\hat{\eta}
, up to scaling by a constant. Recall Assumption
3
on the boundedness of
\frac{t_{n}}{r_{n}}
, where
t_{n}
and
r_{n}
are the upper bound and lower bound on vertex degrees in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
. To verify (A1), note that

\displaystyle\sum_{j}|a_{ij}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\ {\rm or\ }(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}|a_{ij}|+\sum_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{j},Z_{i})\notin\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}|a_{ij}|

\displaystyle\leq 2+\sum_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\ {\rm or\ }(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{2}{r_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq 2+\frac{2t_{n}}{r_{n}}\;=\;O(1)

\displaystyle=O(\max|a_{ij}|\cdot t_{n}),

where in the last line we have used the fact that
\max|a_{ij}|\cdot t_{n}\geq\left(\frac{1}{r_{n}}-\frac{2}{n-1}\right)\cdot t_{n}\geq 1-\frac{2t_{n}}{n-1}\to 1
as
n\to\infty
.
To check (A2), observe that

\displaystyle\sum a_{ij}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i}\sum_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}a_{ij}^{2}

(C.4)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i}\sum_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\left(\frac{1}{d_{i}}-\frac{2}{n-1}\right)^{2}\;=\;\sum_{i}\left(\frac{1}{d_{i}}-\frac{2}{n-1}\right)^{2}d_{i}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\sum_{i}\frac{1}{d_{i}^{2}}\cdot d_{i}\;\geq\;\frac{n}{t_{n}}\;\geq\;\frac{n}{t_{n}}\left(\frac{\max|a_{ij}|}{\frac{2}{r_{n}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{r_{n}}{4}n\,\max a_{ij}^{2}\;\geq\;\frac{1}{4}n\max a_{ij}^{2},

where
x\gtrsim y
means
x\geq Cy
for some fixed
C>0
.
The third line follows as
\max|a_{ij}|\leq\frac{2}{r_{n}}
, and the second
\gtrsim
follows from
\frac{t_{n}}{r_{n}}
being bounded (by Assumption
3
).

To check (B1), we first show that
\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum b_{ij}^{2}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Var}\left[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})\right]
, where
\tilde{\Delta}_{i}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}{\rm Multinoulli}(\pi)
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum b_{ij}^{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{M}\frac{n_{i}n_{j}}{n^{2}}\left(K(i,j)-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{p\neq q}K(\Delta_{p},\Delta_{q})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{n_{i}(n_{i}-1)}{n^{2}}\left(K(i,i)-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{p\neq q}K(\Delta_{p},\Delta_{q})\right)^{2}.

Since
\frac{n_{i}}{n}\to\pi_{i}
and
\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{p\neq q}K(\Delta_{p},\Delta_{q})\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})]
, we have:

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum b_{ij}^{2}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\left(K(i,j)-\mathbb{E}[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})]\right)^{2}={\rm Var}\left[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})\right].

(C.5)

Next, we show

\frac{\sum b_{i+}^{2}}{n^{3}}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{3})-\left(\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})\right)^{2}
. Since
b_{ii}=0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\sum b_{i+}^{2}}{n^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum^{\prime}b_{ij}b_{ik}+\sum b_{ij}^{2}}{n^{3}}.

Similar to the previous argument, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\sum^{\prime}b_{ij}b_{ik}}{n^{3}}

\displaystyle\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\pi_{k}\left(K(i,j)-\mathbb{E}[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})]\right)\left(K(i,k)-\mathbb{E}[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})]\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(K(\tilde{\Delta}_{3},\tilde{\Delta}_{4})-\mathbb{E}[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})]\right)\left(K(\tilde{\Delta}_{3},\tilde{\Delta}_{5})-\mathbb{E}[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})]\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{3})-\left(\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})\right)^{2}.

Together with
\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum b_{ij}^{2}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Var}\left[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})\right]
which implies
\frac{1}{n^{3}}\sum b_{ij}^{2}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}0
, we have:

\frac{\sum b_{i+}^{2}}{n^{3}}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{3})-\left(\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})\right)^{2}.

(C.6)

To verify (B1), it remains to check that

2\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{3})-\left(\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})\right)^{2}}{{\rm Var}\left[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})\right]}<1.

(C.7)

This follows from the fact

\mathbb{E}\left(K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})-\mathbb{E}_{3}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{3})-\mathbb{E}_{3}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{3},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})+\mathbb{E}_{3,4}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{3},\tilde{\Delta}_{4})\right)^{2}>0.

The inequality is strict because otherwise

K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})=f(\tilde{\Delta}_{1})+g(\tilde{\Delta}_{2})
almost surely, which implies

K(p,q)=f(p)+g(q)
for all
p,q\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
, and consequently
\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)x_{p}x_{q}=0
as long as
x_{1}+\ldots+x_{M}=0
, contradicting the fact that
K(\cdot,\cdot)
is characteristic.

To verify (B2), for any
r=3,4,\ldots
,
since the kernel is bounded,
|b_{ij}|^{r}
is also bounded. Together with the fact that

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum b_{ij}^{2}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Var}\left[K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})\right]>0
, we have

\sum|b_{ij}|^{r}/n^{2}=O(1)=O\left((\sum b_{ij}^{2}/n^{2})^{\frac{r}{2}}\right).

Hence all the conditions needed for Theorem
C.1
are satisfied and we have the asymptotic normality of the numerator of
\hat{\eta}
.

Next, we simplify the permutation variance
{\rm Var}(\hat{\eta}|\mathcal{F}_{n})
.
Since if the pooled sample is given, the graph
\mathcal{G}_{n}
is known up to a permutation.
We can arbitrarily fix a labeling of the vertices as
1,\ldots,n
with out-degree
d_{i}
(which is a slight abuse of notation, since
d_{i}
was originally defined as the degree of
Z_{i}
whose label is
\Delta_{i}
).
Let
(\check{\Delta}_{1},\ldots,\check{\Delta}_{n})
be a uniformly random permutation of
({\Delta}_{1},\ldots,{\Delta}_{n})
.
Since the denominator of
\hat{\eta}
is constant, it suffices to compute the variance of

N_{n}=\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{{d}_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\check{\Delta}_{i},\check{\Delta}_{j})-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})\right).

Since
\mathcal{G}_{n}
does not contain any self-loop, for any
i\neq j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
\mathbb{E}[K(\check{\Delta}_{i},\check{\Delta}_{j})]=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{l\neq m}K(\Delta_{l},\Delta_{m})
. This implies
\mathbb{E}[N_{n}]=0
and

{\rm Var}(N_{n})=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{{d}_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\check{\Delta}_{i},\check{\Delta}_{j})\right)^{2}\right]-\frac{1}{n(n-1)^{2}}\left(\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})\right)^{2}.

Let

\displaystyle\tilde{a}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[K^{2}(\check{\Delta}_{1},\check{\Delta}_{2})]=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}{\sum}^{\prime}K^{2}(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}),

\displaystyle\tilde{b}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[K(\check{\Delta}_{1},\check{\Delta}_{2})K(\check{\Delta}_{1},\check{\Delta}_{3})]=\frac{1}{n(n-1)(n-2)}{\sum}^{\prime}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{l}),

\displaystyle\tilde{c}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[K(\check{\Delta}_{1},\check{\Delta}_{2})K(\check{\Delta}_{3},\check{\Delta}_{4})]=\frac{1}{n(n-1)(n-2)(n-3)}{\sum}^{\prime}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})K(\Delta_{l},\Delta_{m}),

where
\sum^{\prime}
means the summation indices are required to be distinct. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{{d}_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\check{\Delta}_{i},\check{\Delta}_{j})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\tilde{a}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d_{i}}{d_{i}^{2}}+\dsum_{(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{j}}\right)

\displaystyle+\tilde{b}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d_{i}(d_{i}-1)}{d_{i}^{2}}+\dsum_{(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{s},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{s}}+\dsum_{(Z_{i},Z_{s}),(Z_{s},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{s}}+\dsum_{(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{s},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{s}}\right)

\displaystyle+\tilde{c}\cdot{\sum}^{\prime}\frac{\left(d_{i}-1_{(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\right)\left(d_{j}-1_{(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\right)-T^{\mathcal{G}_{n}}(i,j)}{d_{i}d_{j}},

where
T^{\mathcal{G}_{n}}(Z_{i},Z_{j})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}I\{(Z_{i},Z_{k}),(Z_{j},Z_{k})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\}
is the number of common out-neighbors of
Z_{i}
and
Z_{j}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\dsum_{(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{s},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{s}}

\displaystyle=\dsum_{(Z_{s},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{d_{i}-1_{(Z_{i},Z_{s})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}}{d_{i}d_{s}},

\displaystyle\dsum_{(Z_{i},Z_{s}),(Z_{s},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{s}}

\displaystyle=\dsum_{(Z_{i},Z_{s})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{d_{s}-1_{(Z_{s},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}}{d_{i}d_{s}},

\displaystyle\dsum_{(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{s},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{s}}

\displaystyle={\sum}^{\prime}\frac{T^{\mathcal{G}_{n}}(Z_{i},Z_{s})}{d_{i}d_{s}}.

Recall that
\tilde{g}_{1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}
,
\tilde{g}_{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{T^{\mathcal{G}_{n}}(i,j)}{d_{i}d_{j}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{\prime}\frac{T^{\mathcal{G}_{n}}(i,j)}{d_{i}d_{j}}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}
, and

\tilde{g}_{3}=\frac{1}{n}\dsum_{(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{j}}
. Hence we have:

\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Bigg(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{{d}_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\check{\Delta}_{i},\check{\Delta}_{j})\Bigg)^{2}\Bigg]=\tilde{a}(\tilde{g}_{1}+\tilde{g}_{3})+\tilde{b}(3-2\tilde{g}_{1}+\tilde{g}_{2}-2\tilde{g}_{3})+\tilde{c}(n-3+\tilde{g}_{1}-\tilde{g}_{2}+\tilde{g}_{3}).

The second term of
{\rm Var}(N_{n})
is easier to handle:

\left(\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})\right)^{2}=n(n-1)(n-2)(n-3)\cdot\tilde{c}+4n(n-1)(n-2)\cdot\tilde{b}+2n(n-1)\cdot\tilde{a}.

Combining the two, we get

{\rm Var}(N_{n})=\tilde{a}\Bigg(\tilde{g}_{1}+\tilde{g}_{3}-\frac{2}{n-1}\Bigg)+\tilde{b}\Bigg(\tilde{g}_{2}-2\tilde{g}_{1}-2\tilde{g}_{3}-1+\frac{4}{n-1}\Bigg)+\tilde{c}\Bigg(\tilde{g}_{1}-\tilde{g}_{2}+\tilde{g}_{3}+\frac{n-3}{n-1}\Bigg).

(C.8)

Hence

\frac{\hat{\eta}}{{\rm Var}(\hat{\eta}|\mathcal{F}_{n})}=\frac{\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{{d}_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K({\Delta}_{i},{\Delta}_{j})-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})\right)}{\tilde{a}\left(\tilde{g}_{1}+\tilde{g}_{3}-\frac{2}{n-1}\right)+\tilde{b}\left(\tilde{g}_{2}-2\tilde{g}_{1}-2\tilde{g}_{3}-1+\frac{4}{n-1}\right)+\tilde{c}\left(\tilde{g}_{1}-\tilde{g}_{2}+\tilde{g}_{3}+\frac{n-3}{n-1}\right)}.

From the conditional CLT, the unconditional CLT also follows:

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\hat{\eta}}{{\rm Var}(\hat{\eta}|\mathcal{F}_{n})}\leq x\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\hat{\eta}}{{\rm Var}(\hat{\eta}|\mathcal{F}_{n})}\leq x|\mathcal{F}_{n}\right)\right]\to\Phi(x),

by the dominated convergence, where
\Phi(x)
is the cumulative distribution function of
N(0,1)
. ∎

C.9
Proof of Corollary
2

Note that
\tilde{a},\tilde{b},\tilde{c}
are bounded as the kernel is bounded.
Further,

\tilde{g}_{1}\leq\frac{1}{r_{n}}\leq 1
,

\tilde{g}_{2}=\frac{1}{n}\dsum\frac{T^{\mathcal{G}_{n}}(i,j)}{d_{i}d_{j}}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\leq\frac{t_{n}^{2}}{r_{n}^{2}}+\frac{1}{r_{n}}=O(1)

since
t_{n}
is an upper bound for the in-degree, and

\tilde{g}_{3}\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{j}}\leq\frac{2t_{n}}{r_{n}^{2}}=O(1).

Hence
{\rm Var}(N_{n})=O(1)
and
therefore
{\rm Var}(\hat{\eta}|\mathcal{F}_{n})=O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
.
If the
M
distributions are not equal, then
\eta>0
, and
\hat{\eta}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\eta
since Assumption
1
holds. Hence:

\frac{\hat{\eta}}{\sqrt{{\rm Var}(\hat{\eta}|\mathcal{F}_{n})}}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}+\infty,

which implies the universal consistency of the test based on
\hat{\eta}
.

C.10
Proof of Theorem
4

The convergence of the denominator of
\hat{\eta}
(in (
4
)) is easy to see, as
n\to\infty
,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{i})-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{i\neq j}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}K(i,i)-\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}K(i,j)>0.

To see why the limit is positive, we write
\tilde{\Delta}_{i}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}{\rm Multinoulli}(\pi)
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, and it is equivalent to showing that

\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{1})-\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})>0
.
Since
K(\cdot,\cdot)
is characteristic, the distance between Dirac measures

{\rm MMD}(\delta_{i},\delta_{j})>0
whenever
i\neq j\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
, so equivalently

K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{1})+K(\tilde{\Delta}_{2},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})-2K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})>0
whenever
\tilde{\Delta}_{1}\neq\tilde{\Delta}_{2}
. After taking the expectation, we have
\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{1})-\mathbb{E}K(\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\tilde{\Delta}_{2})>0
.

The (conditional) variance of
N_{n}
(in Equation
C.8
), i.e., the numerator of
\hat{\eta}
, involves
\tilde{a},\tilde{b},\tilde{c}
and
\tilde{g}_{1},\tilde{g}_{2},\tilde{g}_{3}
. It is clear that:

\displaystyle\tilde{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}{\sum}^{\prime}K^{2}(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}K^{2}(i,j)=a,

\displaystyle\tilde{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-1)(n-2)}{\sum}^{\prime}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{l})\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\sum_{i,j,l=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\pi_{l}K(i,j)K(i,l)=b,

\displaystyle\tilde{c}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-1)(n-2)(n-3)}{\sum}^{\prime}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})K(\Delta_{l},\Delta_{m})

\displaystyle\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\sum_{i,j,l,m=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\pi_{l}\pi_{m}K(i,j)K(l,m)=\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}K(i,j)\right)^{2}=c,\ {\rm as\ }n\to\infty.

To show the convergence of
\tilde{g}_{1},\tilde{g}_{2},\tilde{g}_{3}
,
note that they are functions of
\mathcal{G}(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
, with
\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}\}
being the pooled sample, and
\mathcal{G}
being a stabilizing graph as defined in Section
4.2
. Further, they can all be expressed in terms of
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi(Z_{i},\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}\})
, and thus can be analyzed using the tool of
stabilizing functions
to be introduced next.

Definition C.1

(Stabilizing functions
[
56
]
)
.

Let
\xi(x,\mathcal{X})
be a measurable
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-valued function defined for all finite set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

x\in\mathcal{X}
. If
x\notin\mathcal{X}
, define
\xi(x,\mathcal{X}):=\xi(x,\mathcal{X}\cup\{x\})
.

\xi
is said to be translation invariant, if

\xi(x+y,\mathcal{X}+y)=\xi(x,\mathcal{X})
for
all finite set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For a locally finite
13
13

13

\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to be locally finite if its intersection with any compact set is finite.
set
\mathcal{X}
, if

\displaystyle\liminf_{m\to\infty}\underbrace{\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\mathop{\rm ess\ inf}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash B(\mathbf{0},m)\\
|\mathcal{A}|=n\end{subarray}}\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\big(\mathcal{X}\cap B(\mathbf{0},m)\big)\cup\mathcal{A}\right)\right)}_{{\rm denoted\ by\ }\underline{\xi}(\mathcal{X},m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\limsup_{m\to\infty}\underbrace{\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\mathop{\rm ess\ sup}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash B(\mathbf{0},m)\\
|\mathcal{A}|=n\end{subarray}}\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\big(\mathcal{X}\cap B(\mathbf{0},m)\big)\cup\mathcal{A}\right)\right)}_{{\rm denoted\ by\ }\bar{\xi}(\mathcal{X},m)},

where the essential supremum/infimum is taken with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{dn}
,
then
\xi
is said to stabilize on
\mathcal{X}
.
In such a case, we define
\xi(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{X})
as the limit of the above quantity.

We will be interested in functions that stabilize on the homogeneous Poisson process
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}
, as the pooled sample
\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}\}
is locally close to a homogeneous Poisson process.
These functions arise naturally from stabilizing graphs. For example, if
\mathcal{G}
is stabilizing on
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}
, and
\xi
satisfies, for any finite set
\mathcal{X}
containing
\mathbf{0}
,
\xi(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{X})
only depends on the edge set
\mathcal{E}\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X})\right)
, then
\xi
stabilizes on
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}
.

Theorem C.2

(
[
56
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Suppose
q=1
or
q=2
. Let
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
be i.i.d.
d
-dimensional random variables with common density
f
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}:=\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
. Suppose
\xi
is translation invariant and almost surely stabilizing on the homogeneous Poisson process
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda>0
.
If
\xi
satisfies the moment condition

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}\left[\xi(n^{1/d}X_{1},n^{1/d}\mathcal{X}_{n})^{p}\right]<\infty

for some
p>q
, then as
n\to\infty
,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{n}}\xi(n^{1/d}x,n^{1/d}\mathcal{X}_{n})\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}[\xi(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{f(x)})]f(x){\rm d}x,\quad in\ L^{q}

and the right-hand side above is finite.
Here
\mathcal{P}_{f(x)}
is a homogeneous Poisson process with intensity
f(x)
.

In particular, if
\xi(x,\mathcal{X})
is also scale invariant (i.e.,
\xi(ax,a\mathcal{X})=\xi(x,\mathcal{X})
for all
a>0
), then

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{n}}\xi(x,\mathcal{X}_{n})\to\mathbb{E}[\xi(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{1})],\quad in\ L^{q}.

Let us now get back to the proof of our Theorem
4
. Since the graph
\mathcal{G}
is stabilizing,
\xi(x,\mathcal{X}):=\frac{1}{d(x,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X}))}
is a bounded stabilizing function.
Using the above theorem, by the same coupling used in Appendix
C.7
(the proof of the consistency theorem for
\hat{\eta}
), the limit of
\tilde{g}_{1}
is unchanged when we replace
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
by i.i.d. observations from
\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}P_{i}
:

\tilde{g}_{1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d\big(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\big)}\right]=g_{1}.

By
Penrose
et al. 2003
,
\xi(x,\mathcal{X}):=\sum_{y\neq z:(y,x),(z,x)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X}))}\frac{1}{d\left(y,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X})\right)d\left(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X})\right)}
is also bounded and stabilizing on
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}
for any
\lambda>0
. Hence

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{2}-\tilde{g}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{T^{\mathcal{G}_{n}}(i,j)}{d_{i}d_{j}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq k:(Z_{j},Z_{i}),(Z_{k},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{j}d_{k}}

\displaystyle\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{y\neq z:(y,\mathbf{0}),(z,\mathbf{0})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\frac{1}{d\left(y,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)d\left(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)}\right]=g_{2}-g_{1}.

Similarly, by considering
\xi(x,\mathcal{X}):=\sum_{y:(y,x),(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X}))}\frac{1}{d\left(x,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X})\right)d\left(y,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X})\right)}
, we have

\tilde{g}_{3}=\frac{1}{n}{\sum_{(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{j}}\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{y:(y,\mathbf{0}),(\mathbf{0},y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\frac{1}{d\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)d\left(y,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)}\right]=g_{3}.

Hence the (conditional) variance of the numerator of
\hat{\eta}
, scaled by
\sqrt{n}
, converges in
L^{2}
to

\displaystyle{\displaystyle a}\left({g}_{1}+{g}_{3}\right)+{b}\left({g}_{2}-2{g}_{1}-2{g}_{3}-1\right)+{c}\left({g}_{1}-{g}_{2}+{g}_{3}+1\right)

\displaystyle=(a-2b+c)(g_{1}+g_{3})+(g_{2}-1)(b-c).

To show the limiting variance is positive, note that from (
C.7
),
a-2b+c>0
, and from (
C.6
),
b\geq c
.
Hence it remains to show
g_{2}\geq 1
. Note that

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{T^{\mathcal{G}_{n}}(i,j)}{d_{i}d_{j}}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq l:(Z_{j},Z_{i}),(Z_{l},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{j}d_{l}}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j:(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j:(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{j}^{2}}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j:(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j:(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\right)^{2}=1,

where in the last step we have used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
Hence
g_{2}
, as the
L^{2}
limit of
\tilde{g}_{2}
, is greater than or equal to 1. ∎

C.11
Proof of Proposition
B.1

Recall
\phi=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}f_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{O}
be the interior of
{\rm supp}(\phi)
— a set with probability 1, under
\phi
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq I
, be a finite collection of infinite open cones covering
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with angular radius
\pi/12
and apex at
\mathbf{0}
.
For
x\in\mathcal{O}
and
1\leq i\leq I
, let
\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)
be the translate of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
with apex at
x
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}(x)
be the open cone concentric to
\mathcal{C}_{i}
with apex
x
and angular radius
\pi/6
.
For
1\leq i\leq I
, let
N^{-1/d}R_{i}(x)
be the distance from
x
to its
k
-th nearest neighbor in
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}\cap\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}(x)
, if this
k
-th nearest neighbor exists at a distance less than
{\rm diam}(\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\cap\mathcal{O})
, and otherwise set
N^{-1/d}R_{i}(x)={\rm diam}(\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\cap\mathcal{O})
.
Then
\max_{1\leq i\leq I}R_{i}(x)
is a radius of stabilization.
Note that
\mathbb{P}\left[R_{i}(x)>t\right]
is 0 unless
{\rm diam}(\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\cap\mathcal{O})>N^{-1/d}t
.
Now, consider
{\rm diam}(\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\cap\mathcal{O})\geq N^{-1/d}t
. Then there exists a
y\in\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\cap\mathcal{O}
such that
|y-x|=N^{-1/d}t
. By convexity,
\frac{x+y}{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\cap\mathcal{O}
.
With
\gamma:=\frac{1}{2}\sin(\pi/12)
, we have

B(\frac{x+y}{2},\gamma N^{-1/d}t)\subset\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{O}
.

R_{i}(x)>t
implies that
B(\frac{x+y}{2},\gamma N^{-1/d}t)
contains less than
k
points.
Since
\phi_{N}
is bounded below,
the number of points in
B(\frac{x+y}{2},\gamma N^{-1/d}t)
is a Poisson random variable with mean at least

N\int_{B(\frac{x+y}{2},\gamma N^{-1/d}t)}\inf\{\phi_{N}\}{\rm d}z=\delta t^{d}
for some
\delta>0
independent of
i,x,t,N
. Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[R_{i}>t]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[{\rm Poisson}\left(\delta t^{d}\right)<k\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{(\delta t^{d})^{i}}{i!}e^{-\delta t^{d}}\leq C_{1}e^{-C_{2}t^{d}},

where
C_{1},C_{2}>0
are independent of
i,x,t,N
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\tau(t)

\displaystyle=\sup_{N\geq 1,x\in\mathcal{O}}\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{1\leq i\leq I}R_{i}>t\right]\leq IC_{1}e^{-C_{2}t^{d}}.

which yields the desired result. ∎

C.12
Proof of Theorem
B.1

We first provide a roadmap for the proof. Recall the definitions of
H_{n}
,
\mathcal{F}_{n}
,
\{\kappa_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2}
in Appendix
B.1
.
In the following, we first provide some lemmas that describe the local behavior of
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
.
Next, in Step 1 of the main proof, we show
H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\mid\mathcal{F}_{n}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{1}^{2})
, where the provided lemmas are crucial in establishing the convergence of the variance, and the CLT is established using Stein’s method.
Next, in Step 2, we show that

\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{2}^{2})
.
This is the step where we use
\sqrt{N}\left(\frac{N_{p}}{N}-\pi_{p}\right)\to 0
and the power-law stabilization. To establish the convergence of variance, we need power-law stabilization of order
q>d
(recall that
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
), and to establish the CLT using Stein’s method, power-law stabilization of order
q>16d
is needed.
Finally, combining Step 1 and Step 2, we can show that
H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2})
.

C.12.1
Preliminaries

The following four lemmas are modifications of Lemma 3.1, Lemma 3.2, and Proposition 3.1 from
Penrose
et al. 2003
, which describe that the local behavior of
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
at
z
is similar to a homogeneous Poisson process
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(z)}
, where
\phi_{N}(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{N_{i}}{N}f_{i}(z)
, for
z\in\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
\frac{N_{i}}{N}\to\pi_{i}
as
N\to\infty
, and
\phi(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}f_{i}(z)
.

We say that
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a
Lebesgue point
of a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, if

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{B(x,r)}|f(y)-f(x)|{\rm d}y=0.

If
x
is a continuity point of
f
, then
x
is a Lebesgue point of
f
. More generally, the
Lebesgue differentiation theorem
states that given
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, a.e.
x
is a Lebesgue point of
f
.

Lemma C.1
.

Recall that
\phi(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}f_{i}(z)
, for
z\in\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Suppose
x_{0}
is a Lebesgue point of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{M}
. Then there exists a homogeneous Poisson process
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
, coupled with
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
, such that for all
K>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left[N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}-x_{0})\cap B(\mathbf{0},K)=\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\cap B(\mathbf{0},K)\right]\to 1,\quad{\rm as\ }N\to\infty.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a homogeneous Poisson process of rate 1 on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,\infty)
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
be the projection of the set

\left\{(x,t)\in\mathcal{P}:t\leq N\phi_{N}(x)\right\}

onto the
x
space, i.e.,
(x,t)\mapsto x
.
Then
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
is a non-homogeneous Poisson process with intensity function
N\phi_{N}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
be the image of the point set

\{(x,t)\in\mathcal{P}:t\leq N\phi(x_{0})\}

under the mapping

(x,t)\mapsto N^{1/d}(x-x_{0}).

Note that
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
is a homogeneous Poisson process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with intensity
\phi(x_{0})
for all
N
.
The number of points in

\left(N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}-x_{0})\triangle\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\right)\cap B(\mathbf{0},K)

equals the number of points
(x,t)\in\mathcal{P}
such that
x\in B(x_{0},N^{-1/d}K)
and
t
is between
\phi_{N}(x)
and
\phi(x_{0})
, and follows a Poisson distribution with mean

N\int_{B(x_{0},N^{-1/d}K)}|\phi_{N}(x)-\phi(x_{0})|{\rm d}x\leq N\int_{B(x_{0},N^{-1/d}K)}(|\phi_{N}(x)-\phi(x)|+|\phi(x)-\phi(x_{0})|){\rm d}x,

(C.9)

which converges to 0 as
x_{0}
is also a Lebesgue point of
\phi
.
∎

Lemma C.2
.

Suppose
x_{0}
is a Lebesgue point of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{M}
and
\phi(x_{0})>0
. Let
\xi(x,\mathcal{X})
be a translation invariant function that almost surely stabilizes on
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
(see Definition
C.1
). Suppose the moment condition

\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\xi\left(\mathbf{0},N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}-x_{0})\right)\right|^{p}\right]<\infty

is satisfied for some
p>1
. Then

\xi\left(\mathbf{0},N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}-x_{0})\right)\to\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\right)

in distribution and in expectation.

Proof.

Consider the coupling of
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
in the previous lemma. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\xi\left(\mathbf{0},N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}-x_{0})\right)-\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\right)\right|>\varepsilon\right]
