\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big[N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}-x_{0})\cap B(\mathbf{0},K)\neq\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\cap B(\mathbf{0},K)\big]+\mathbb{P}\big[\bar{\xi}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})},K)-\underline{\xi}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})},K)\geq\varepsilon\big],

where
\bar{\xi}
and
\underline{\xi}
are defined in Definition
C.1
.
By the stabilization assumption, we can choose
K>0
so that second term is less than
\delta
, for any
\delta>0
, by taking
K
large. By the previous lemma, the first term converges to 0 as
N\to\infty
. Since
\delta>0
is arbitrary, it follows that

\xi\left(\mathbf{0},N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}-x_{0})\right)\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\right).

The assumption on the boundedness of the
p
-th moment implies that
\{\xi\left(\mathbf{0},N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}-x_{0})\right)\}_{N\geq 1}
is uniformly integrable; hence its expectation converges to the expectation of
\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\right)
.
∎

Lemma C.3
.

Suppose
x_{0}\neq y_{0}
are Lebesgue points of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{M}
.
There exist homogeneous Poisson processes
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}
coupled with
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
is independent of
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}
and for all
K>0
:

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y_{0}}-x_{0})\cap B(\mathbf{0},K)=\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\cap B(\mathbf{0},K)\right]\ =1,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x_{0}}-y_{0})\cap B(\mathbf{0},K)=\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}\cap B(\mathbf{0},K)\right]\ =1.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a homogeneous Poisson processes of rate 1 on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,\infty)
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
be the projection of the set

\left\{(x,t)\in\mathcal{P}:t\leq N\phi_{N}(x)\right\}

onto the
x
space, i.e.,
(x,t)\mapsto x
.
Let
\mathcal{Q}
be an independent copy of
\mathcal{P}
.
Let
F_{x_{0}}:=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|x_{0}-z\|<\|y_{0}-z\|\}
be the half-space of points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
closer to
x_{0}
than to
y_{0}
and let
F_{y_{0}}:=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|y_{0}-z\|<\|x_{0}-z\|\}
be the half-space of points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
closer to
y_{0}
than to
x_{0}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
be the image of the point set

\{(x,t)\in\mathcal{P}\cap F_{x_{0}}\times[0,N\phi(x_{0})]\}\cup\{(x,t)\in\mathcal{Q}\cap F_{y_{0}}\times[0,N\phi(x_{0})]\}

under the mapping

(x,t)\mapsto N^{1/d}(x-x_{0}).

Note that
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
is a homogeneous Poisson process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with intensity
\phi(x_{0})
for all
N
.
Note that when
N
is large,
B(x_{0},N^{-1/d}K)\subset F_{x_{0}}
.
Hence, the number of points in

\left(N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y_{0}}-x_{0})\triangle\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\right)\cap B(\mathbf{0},K)

equals the number of points
(x,t)\in\mathcal{P}
such that
x\in B(x_{0},N^{-1/d}K)
and
t
is between
\phi_{N}(x)
and
\phi(x_{0})
, and follows a Poisson distribution with mean

N\int_{B(x_{0},N^{-1/d}K)}|\phi_{N}(x)-\phi(x_{0})|{\rm d}x\leq N\int_{B(x_{0},N^{-1/d}K)}(|\phi_{N}(x)-\phi(x)|+|\phi(x)-\phi(x_{0})|){\rm d}x,

which converges to 0 as
x_{0}
is also a Lebesgue point of
\phi
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}
be the image of the point set

\{(x,t)\in\mathcal{P}\cap F_{y_{0}}\times[0,N\phi(y_{0})]\}\cup\{(x,t)\in\mathcal{Q}\cap F_{x_{0}}\times[0,N\phi(y_{0})]\}

under the mapping

(x,t)\mapsto N^{1/d}(x-y_{0}).

\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}
are independent as they are constructed from Poisson processes on disjoint regions of space. The number of points in

\left(N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x_{0}}-y_{0})\triangle\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}\right)\cap B(\mathbf{0},K)
also converges in mean to 0 since it follows a Poisson distribution with mean

N\int_{B(y_{0},N^{-1/d}K)}|\phi_{N}(x)-\phi(y_{0})|{\rm d}x\leq N\int_{B(y_{0},N^{-1/d}K)}(|\phi_{N}(x)-\phi(x)|+|\phi(x)-\phi(y_{0})|){\rm d}x,

which converges to 0 as
y_{0}
is also a Lebesgue point of
\phi
.
∎

Lemma C.4
.

Suppose
x_{0}\neq y_{0}
are Lebesgue points of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{M}
and
\phi(x_{0}),\phi(y_{0})>0
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}
be independent homogeneous Poisson processes with intensity
\phi(x_{0})
and
\phi(y_{0})
respectively.
Suppose
\xi
is translation invariant, almost surely stabilizing on
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}
.
Suppose the moment condition:

\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\xi\left(x_{0},x_{0}+N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y_{0}}-x_{0})\right)\xi\left(y_{0},y_{0}+N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x_{0}}-y_{0})\right)\right|^{p}\right]<\infty

is satisfied for some
p>1
. Then:

\xi\left(x_{0},x_{0}+N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y_{0}}-x_{0})\right)\xi\left(y_{0},y_{0}+N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x_{0}}-y_{0})\right)\to\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\right)\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}\right)

in distribution and in expectation.

Proof.

Consider the coupling of
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})},\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}
in the previous lemma. The same argument in Lemma
C.2
shows

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\xi\left(x_{0},x_{0}+N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y_{0}}-x_{0})\right)-\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\right)\right|>\varepsilon\right]\to 0,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\xi\left(y_{0},y_{0}+N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x_{0}}-y_{0})\right)-\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}\right)\right|>\varepsilon\right]\to 0.

Hence

\xi\left(x_{0},x_{0}+N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y_{0}}-x_{0})\right)\xi\left(y_{0},y_{0}+N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x_{0}}-y_{0})\right)\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x_{0})}\right)\xi\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y_{0})}\right).

Convergence in expectation again follows from uniform integrability.
∎

Lemma C.5

(Palm theory for Poisson processes
[
53
, Theorem 1.6]
)
.

Suppose
N>0
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, and
h(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X})
is a bounded measurable function defined on all pairs of the form
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X})
where
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is finite, and
\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathcal{X}
, satisfying
h(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X})=0
when
\mathcal{Y}
does not contain
j
elements. Then

\mathbb{E}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathcal{P}_{Nf}}h(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{P}_{Nf})=\frac{N^{j}}{j!}\mathbb{E}h(x_{j}^{\prime},x_{j}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{P}_{Nf}),

where the sum on the left-hand side is over all subsets
\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathcal{P}_{Nf}
, and on the right-hand side
x_{j}^{\prime}
is a set of
j
i.i.d. observations from
f
, independent of
\mathcal{P}_{Nf}
.

C.12.2
Proof of Theorem
B.1

Write
g_{N}(\Delta_{i}):=2\sum_{p=1}^{M}\frac{N_{p}}{N}K(\Delta_{i},p)-\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}\frac{N_{p}N_{q}}{N^{2}}K(p,q)
. By standard U-statistics projection theory
[
21
, Lemma D.4]
,
there exists a constant
C
such that for
n\geq 2
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{N}(\Delta_{i})\right)^{2}\Big|n\right]\leq\frac{C}{n}.

Since
n\sim{\rm Poisson}(N)
,
\mathbb{P}(n<2)
decays exponentially as
N\to\infty
, and recall that we set
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})
to 0 when
n<2
. This implies

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{N}(\Delta_{i})\right)^{2}\right]\to 0,\ {\rm as\ }N\to\infty.

Hence
\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i\neq j}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{N}(\Delta_{i})+o_{p}(\sqrt{n})
.
It suffices to derive the asymptotic distribution of

H_{n}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{N}(\Delta_{i}).

(C.10)

Let
\mathcal{F}_{n}=\sigma(n,Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the unlabelled data and the number of total observations. The strategy of the proof goes as follows:
First, we show that given
\mathcal{F}_{n}
,
H_{n}
— centered by its conditional mean — converges conditionally to a normal distribution with constant variance, i.e.,
H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\mid\mathcal{F}_{n}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{1}^{2})
.
Second, we show that
\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{2}^{2})
.
Finally, a simple argument using the characteristic function yields that
H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2})
. Observe that, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{it\left(H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{it\left(H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right)}e^{it\left(\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[e^{it\left(H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right)}\big|\mathcal{F}_{n}\right]e^{it\left(\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\right)}\right].

Note that for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, by continuous mapping,

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{it\left(H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right)}\big|\mathcal{F}_{n}\right]e^{it\left(\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\right)}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{1}^{2}t}\cdot e^{it\cdot N(0,\kappa_{2}^{2})}.

Hence, by choosing an almost sure representative and applying the dominated convergence theorem, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[e^{it\left(H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right)}\big|\mathcal{F}_{n}\right]e^{it\left(\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\right)}\right]\to\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{1}^{2}t}\cdot e^{it\cdot N(0,\kappa_{2}^{2})}\right]=e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2})t}.

This implies
H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2})
.

Step 1.
We wil first show that
H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\mid\mathcal{F}_{n}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{1}^{2})
.

We first compute the limiting variance of
H_{n}
given
\mathcal{F}_{n}
. Observe that

\displaystyle{\rm Var}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\neq j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}^{2}}{\rm Var}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})|\mathcal{F}_{n})

(C.11a)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\neq j:(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{j}}{\rm Var}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})|\mathcal{F}_{n})

(C.11b)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,l{\rm\ distinct:}\\
(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{i},Z_{l})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{d_{i}^{2}}{\rm Cov}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}),K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{l})|\mathcal{F}_{n})

(C.11c)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,l{\rm\ distinct:}\\
(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{j},Z_{l})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{j}}{\rm Cov}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}),K(\Delta_{j},\Delta_{l})|\mathcal{F}_{n})

(C.11d)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,l{\rm\ distinct:}\\
(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{l},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{l}}{\rm Cov}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}),K(\Delta_{l},\Delta_{i})|\mathcal{F}_{n})

(C.11e)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,l{\rm\ distinct:}\\
(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{l},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{l}}{\rm Cov}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}),K(\Delta_{l},\Delta_{j})|\mathcal{F}_{n})

(C.11f)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{2}{N}\sum_{i\neq j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}}{\rm Cov}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}),g_{N}(\Delta_{i})|\mathcal{F}_{n})

(C.11g)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{2}{N}\sum_{i\neq j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}}{\rm Cov}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}),g_{N}(\Delta_{j})|\mathcal{F}_{n})

(C.11h)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\rm Var}(g_{N}(\Delta_{i})|\mathcal{F}_{n}).

(C.11i)

We will show the convergence of each term above.
Let
\phi_{N}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{N_{i}}{N}f_{i}(z)
be the marginal density of
Z
, and

\phi(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}f_{i}(z)
be its limit.
Then, for
p\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(z)}{\phi_{N}(z)}-\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(z)}{\phi(z)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(z)\phi(z)-\pi_{p}f_{p}(z)\phi_{N}(z)}{\phi(z)\phi_{N}(z)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(z)\pi_{i}f_{i}(z)-\pi_{p}f_{p}(z)\frac{N_{i}}{N}f_{i}(z)}{\phi(z)\phi_{N}(z)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{|\frac{N_{p}}{N}-\pi_{p}|f_{p}(z)\pi_{i}f_{i}(z)+\pi_{p}f_{p}(z)|\pi_{i}-\frac{N_{i}}{N}|f_{i}(z)}{\phi(z)\phi_{N}(z)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\left(\frac{|\frac{N_{p}}{N}-\pi_{p}|}{\frac{N_{p}}{N}}\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(z)}{\phi_{N}(z)}\frac{\pi_{i}f_{i}(z)}{\phi(z)}+\frac{|\pi_{i}-\frac{N_{i}}{N}|}{\frac{N_{i}}{N}}\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(z)}{\phi(z)}\frac{\frac{N_{i}}{N}f_{i}(z)}{\phi_{N}(z)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{M}\left(\frac{|\frac{N_{p}}{N}-\pi_{p}|}{\frac{N_{p}}{N}}+\frac{|\pi_{i}-\frac{N_{i}}{N}|}{\frac{N_{i}}{N}}\right)\to 0

uniformly over
z\in\{z:\phi(z)>0\}
. Observe that

{\rm Var}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})|\mathcal{F}_{n})=\underbrace{\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K^{2}(p,q)\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{i})}\frac{\frac{N_{q}}{N}f_{q}(Z_{j})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{j})}-\left(\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{i})}\frac{\frac{N_{q}}{N}f_{q}(Z_{j})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{j})}\right)^{2}}_{{\rm denoted\ by\ }h_{N}(Z_{i},Z_{j})}.

By the uniform convergence of
\frac{\frac{N_{i}}{N}f_{i}(z)}{\phi_{N}(z)}
to
\frac{\pi_{i}f_{i}(z)}{\phi(z)}
, we know that
h_{N}
converges uniformly to
h
, a bounded function defined by:

h(Z_{i},Z_{j}):=\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K^{2}(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi(Z_{i})}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(Z_{j})}{\phi(Z_{j})}-\left(\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi(Z_{i})}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(Z_{j})}{\phi(Z_{j})}\right)^{2}.

To show the convergence of the first term in the expansion of
{\rm Var}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})
, i.e.,

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\neq j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}^{2}}\cdot h_{N}(Z_{i},Z_{j})=:f(Z),

where
Z:=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
.
We will show that there exists a constant
\mu
such that
\mathbb{E}f(Z)\to\mu
and
\mathbb{E}f^{2}(Z)\to\mu^{2}
, which implies
f(Z)\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\mu
.

By Palm theory (Lemma
C.5
),

\mathbb{E}f(Z)=\int\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d^{2}(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\sum_{y:(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}h_{N}(z,y)\right]\phi_{N}(z){\rm d}z.

(C.12)

To show the convergence of the integrand, we can first replace
h_{N}
by its uniform limit
h
.

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d^{2}(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\sum_{y:(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}h_{N}(z,y)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d^{2}(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\sum_{y:(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}h(z,y)\right].

Note that
(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))
implies that
y
is a neighbor of
z
, and Lemma
C.1
together with the graph being stabilizing on
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(z)}
implies
\sup_{y:(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\|y-z\|=O_{p}\left(N^{-1/d}\right)
.
If
z
is a continuity point of
f_{1},\cdots,f_{M}
, then it is also a continuity point of
h(z,\cdot)
. Hence we can further replace
y
by
z
to get

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d^{2}(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\sum_{y:(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}h(z,y)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d^{2}(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\sum_{y:(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}h(z,z)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\right]h(z,z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi(z)}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]h(z,z)=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]h(z,z).

The last line follows from Lemma
C.2
and
\mathcal{G}
being translation and scale invariant.
Now, by dominated convergence,

\mathbb{E}f(Z)\to\int\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]h(z,z)\phi(z){\rm d}z.

Now we consider
\mathbb{E}f^{2}(Z)
. Write
\xi(x,\mathcal{X}):=\sum_{y:(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X}\cup\{x\}))}\frac{1}{d_{x}^{2}}h_{N}(x,y)
.
Then
f(Z)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})
, and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}f^{2}(Z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}\xi^{2}(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\right]+\frac{2}{N^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\right].

Here
\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}
means summing over all
\frac{n(n-1)}{2}
size-2 subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
. The first term converges to 0 since
\xi
is bounded and the number of points in
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
follows
{\rm Poisson}(N)
. The second term, by Palm theory, equals

\int\mathbb{E}\left[\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y})\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})\right]\phi_{N}(x)\phi_{N}(y){\rm d}x{\rm d}y.

By the uniform convergence of
h_{N}
to
h
, assuming
x,y
are continuity point of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{M}
, the integrand has a limit given by

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y})\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{w:(x,w)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{1}{d_{x}^{2}}h_{N}(x,w)\sum_{w:(y,w)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{1}{d_{y}^{2}}h_{N}(y,w)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{w:(x,w)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{1}{d_{x}^{2}}h(x,x)\sum_{w:(y,w)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{1}{d_{y}^{2}}h(y,y)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d(x,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{1}{d(y,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\right]h(x,x)h(y,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x)}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y)}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]h(x,x)h(y,y)=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]^{2}h(x,x)h(y,y).

The last line follows from Lemma
C.4
and
\mathcal{G}
being translation and scale invariant.
Now, by dominated convergence,

\mathbb{E}f^{2}(Z)\to\int\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]^{2}h(x,x)h(y,y)\phi(x)\phi(y){\rm d}x{\rm d}y.

Hence
f(Z)
converges in
L^{2}
to
\int\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]h(z,z)\phi(z){\rm d}z
.

With the distribution of
\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{Z}
defined in Section
2.1
,
if
\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}
are independently drawn from the conditional distribution of
\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}=z
, then
h(z,z)={\rm Var}[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z]
. Hence the first term in
{\rm Var}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})
converges to:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\neq j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}^{2}}{\rm Var}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})|\mathcal{F}_{n})\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]\int{\rm Var}[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z]\phi(z){\rm d}z.

The same strategy applied to the second term (
C.11b
) yields:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\neq j:(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{j},Z_{i})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{i}d_{j}}{\rm Var}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})|\mathcal{F}_{n})

\displaystyle\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{y:(y,\mathbf{0}),(\mathbf{0},y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\frac{1}{d\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)d\left(y,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)}\right]\int{\rm Var}[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z]\phi(z){\rm d}z.

The convergence of the third term (
C.11c
) can be handled similarly. Note that

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Cov}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}),K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{l})|\mathcal{F}_{n})=\sum_{p,q,r=1}^{M}K(p,q)K(p,r)\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{i})}\frac{\frac{N_{q}}{N}f_{q}(Z_{j})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{j})}\frac{\frac{N_{r}}{N}f_{r}(Z_{l})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{l})}

\displaystyle-\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{i})}\frac{\frac{N_{q}}{N}f_{q}(Z_{j})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{j})}\sum_{p,r=1}^{M}K(p,r)\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{i})}\frac{\frac{N_{r}}{N}f_{r}(Z_{l})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{l})}.

Denote the right-hand side of the above display by
h_{N}(Z_{i},Z_{j},Z_{l})
.
Then
h_{N}
converges uniformly to
h
defined as:

\displaystyle h(Z_{i},Z_{j},Z_{l})=\sum_{p,q,r=1}^{M}K(p,q)K(p,r)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi(Z_{i})}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(Z_{j})}{\phi(Z_{j})}\frac{\pi_{r}f_{r}(Z_{l})}{\phi(Z_{l})}

\displaystyle-\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi(Z_{i})}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(Z_{j})}{\phi(Z_{j})}\sum_{p,r=1}^{M}K(p,r)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi(Z_{i})}\frac{\pi_{r}f_{r}(Z_{l})}{\phi(Z_{l})}.

With
\xi(z,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}):=\frac{1}{d_{x}^{2}}\sum_{x\neq y:(z,x),(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}))}h_{N}(z,x,y)
, (
C.11c
) can be written as

f(Z)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}\xi(z,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}).

By Palm theory,

\mathbb{E}f(Z)=\int\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d^{2}(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\sum_{x\neq y:(z,x),(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}h_{N}(z,x,y)\right]\phi_{N}(z){\rm d}z.

(C.13)

Since
h_{N}
converges uniformly to
h
, using the same argument as before, if
z
is a continuity point of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{M}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d^{2}(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\sum_{x\neq y:(z,x),(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}h_{N}(z,x,y)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d^{2}(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\sum_{x\neq y:(z,x),(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}h(z,x,y)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d^{2}(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\sum_{x\neq y:(z,x),(z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}h(z,z,z)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{d(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))(d(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))-1)}{d^{2}(z,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))}\right]h(z,z,z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[1-\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi(z)}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]h(z,z,z)=\mathbb{E}\left[1-\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]h(z,z,z).

Hence by dominated convergence theorem applied to (
C.13
),

\mathbb{E}f(Z)\to\int\mathbb{E}\left[1-\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]h(z,z,z)\phi(z){\rm d}z.

Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}f^{2}(Z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}\xi^{2}(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\right]+\frac{2}{N^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\right].

The first term converges to 0 again as
\xi
is bounded and the number of points in
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
follows
{\rm Poisson}(N)
. The second term equals

\int\mathbb{E}\left[\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y})\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})\right]\phi_{N}(x)\phi_{N}(y){\rm d}x{\rm d}y
by Palm theory.
By the uniform convergence of
h_{N}
to
h
, assuming
x,y
are continuity point of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{M}
, the integrand has a limit given by

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y})\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\neq v:(x,u),(x,v)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{1}{d_{x}^{2}}h_{N}(x,u,v)\sum_{u\neq v:(y,u),(y,v)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{1}{d_{y}^{2}}h_{N}(y,u,v)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\neq v:(x,u),(x,v)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{1}{d_{x}^{2}}h(x,u,v)\sum_{u\neq v:(y,u),(y,v)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{1}{d_{y}^{2}}h(y,u,v)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\neq v:(x,u),(x,v)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{1}{d_{x}^{2}}h(x,x,x)\sum_{u\neq v:(y,u),(y,v)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{1}{d_{y}^{2}}h(y,y,y)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{d(x,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))-1}{d(x,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\frac{d(y,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))-1}{d(y,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,y}))}\right]h(x,x,x)h(y,y,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x)}^{\mathbf{0}}))-1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x)}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\frac{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y)}^{\mathbf{0}}))-1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi(y)}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]h(x,x,x)h(y,y,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))-1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]^{2}h(x,x,x)h(y,y,y).

The last line again follows from Lemma
C.4
and
\mathcal{G}
being translation and scale invariant.
Now, by dominated convergence,

\mathbb{E}f^{2}(Z)\to\int\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))-1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]^{2}h(x,x,x)h(y,y,y)\phi(x)\phi(y){\rm d}x{\rm d}y.

Hence
f(Z)
converges in
L^{2}
to
\int\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))-1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]h(x,x,x)\phi(x){\rm d}x
.

Therefore, (
C.11c
) converges in
L^{2}
to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,l{\rm\ distinct:}\\
(Z_{i},Z_{j}),(Z_{i},Z_{l})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{d_{i}^{2}}{\rm Cov}(K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}),K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{l})|\mathcal{F}_{n})

\displaystyle\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\left[1-\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]\int h(z,z,z)\phi(z){\rm d}z

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[1-\frac{1}{d(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\right]\int{\rm Cov}(K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}),K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z)\phi(z){\rm d}z,

where
\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}
are independently drawn from the conditional distribution of
\tilde{\Delta}\mid\tilde{Z}=z
.

The other terms in the right side of (
C.11
) converge similarly:

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:subeq4}\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x\neq y:(x,\mathbf{0}),(\mathbf{0},y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\frac{1}{d\left(x,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)d\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)}\right]\int{\rm Cov}(K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}),K(\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z)\phi(z){\rm d}z,

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:subeq5}\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x\neq y:(\mathbf{0},x),(y,\mathbf{0})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\frac{1}{d\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)d\left(y,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)}\right]\int{\rm Cov}(K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}),K(\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime},\tilde{\Delta})|\tilde{Z}=z)\phi(z){\rm d}z,

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:subeq6}\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x\neq y:(x,\mathbf{0}),(y,\mathbf{0})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\frac{1}{d\left(x,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)d\left(y,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)}\right]\int{\rm Cov}(K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}),K(\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z)\phi(z){\rm d}z,

\eqref{eq:subeq7}\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}-2\int{\rm Cov}(K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}),g(\tilde{\Delta})|\tilde{Z}=z)\phi(z){\rm d}z,

\eqref{eq:subeq8}\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}-2\int{\rm Cov}(K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}),g(\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z)\phi(z){\rm d}z,

\eqref{eq:subeq9}\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\left[{\rm Var}(g(\tilde{\Delta})|\tilde{Z})\right].

where
g(\Delta)=2\sum_{p=1}^{M}\pi_{p}K(\Delta,p)-\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}\pi_{p}\pi_{q}K(p,q)
is the uniform limit in
\Delta
of
g_{N}(\Delta)
.

Combining the results above, we have
{\rm Var}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\overset{L^{2}}{\longrightarrow}\kappa_{1}^{2}
, where

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=(g_{1}+g_{3})\int{\rm Var}[K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z]\phi(z){\rm d}z

\displaystyle+(3-2g_{1}-2g_{3}+g_{2})\int{\rm Cov}(K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}),K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z)\phi(z){\rm d}z

\displaystyle-4\int{\rm Cov}(K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}),g(\tilde{\Delta})|\tilde{Z}=z)\phi(z){\rm d}z\;+\;\mathbb{E}\left[{\rm Var}(g(\tilde{\Delta})|\tilde{Z})\right],

where when simplifying the coefficient of
\int{\rm Cov}(K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}),K(\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime})|\tilde{Z}=z)\phi(z){\rm d}z
, we have used that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x\neq y:(x,\mathbf{0}),(\mathbf{0},y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\frac{1}{d\left(x,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)d\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x:(x,\mathbf{0})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\frac{d\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)-1_{(\mathbf{0},x),(x,\mathbf{0})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}}{d\left(x,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)d\left(\mathbf{0},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)}\right],

and

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x:(x,\mathbf{0})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}))}\frac{1}{d\left(x,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}})\right)}\right]=1

since it is the limit of the expectation of
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{n}}\sum_{y:(y,x)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\frac{1}{d_{y}}\equiv 1

[
56
, Lemma 3.2]
. In particular, if the null hypothesis holds true (in which case
\tilde{\Delta}\perp\!\!\!\perp\tilde{Z}
), then
\kappa_{1}^{2}
reduces exactly to the asymptotic variance of the numerator derived in Theorem
4
, which is distribution-free.

If
\kappa_{1}^{2}=0
, then
H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\mid\mathcal{F}_{n}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{1}^{2})
follows trivially from the convergence of the conditional variance of
H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})
to 0. We will assume
\kappa_{1}^{2}>0
and prove the convergence using Stein’s
method based on dependency graphs (see Theorem
C.3
below). First note that
H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}
, where

\displaystyle V_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}g_{N}(\Delta_{i})

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}g_{N}(\Delta_{i})\Big|\mathcal{F}_{n}\right].

Construct a graph
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
on
\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}\}
as follows: for
i\neq j
, there is an edge between
V_{i}
and
V_{j}
in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
if and only if there is a path of length
\leq 2
joining
Z_{i}
and
Z_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
(ignoring the direction of edges in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
).
Then for any pair of disjoint sets
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\subset\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}\}
such that no edge in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
has one endpoint in
\Gamma_{1}
and the other in
\Gamma_{2}
,
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in\Gamma_{1}}
is independent of
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in\Gamma_{2}}
conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
. This implies that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
is a dependency graph
[
18
]
.
Suppose
t_{n}
is the maximum degree of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
.
Then the maximal degree in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
has an upper bound of
t_{n}^{2}
.
We will use the following CLT for dependency graph (with
p=3
).

Theorem C.3

(
18
, Theorem 2.7
)
.

Suppose
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{V}}
are random variables indexed by vertices of a dependency graph, whose maximal degree is
D
. Set
W=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{V}}X_{i}
. If
{\rm Var}(W)=1
,
\mathbb{E}X_{i}=0
, and
\mathbb{E}|X_{i}|^{p}\leq\theta^{p}
for
i\in\mathcal{V}
, then we have

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathbb{P}(W\leq z)-\Phi(z)|\leq 75D^{5(p-1)}|\mathcal{V}|\theta^{p}.

Note that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}g_{N}(\Delta_{i})\right|^{3}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})\right|^{3}+\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}g_{N}(\Delta_{i})\right|^{3}\right)(1+1)(1+1)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\|K\|_{\infty}\right|^{3}+\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\|g_{N}\|_{\infty}\right|^{3}\right)\;\lesssim\;\frac{1}{N^{3/2}}.

Hence

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{H_{n}-\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})}{{\rm Var}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})}\leq z\Big|\mathcal{F}_{n}\right)-\Phi(z)\right|

\displaystyle=\;\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{V_{i}}{{\rm Var}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})}\leq z\Big|\mathcal{F}_{n}\right)-\Phi(z)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim 75D^{5(3-1)}\cdot n\cdot\frac{1}{N^{3/2}({\rm Var}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n}))^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{75t_{n}^{20}}{n^{1/2}({\rm Var}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n}))^{3}}\cdot\frac{n^{3/2}}{N^{3/2}}\overset{p}{\to}0.

Step 2.
We will now show that
\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{2}^{2})
.

Note that
\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})
can be written as:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{i})}\frac{\frac{N_{q}}{N}f_{q}(Z_{j})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{j})}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{p=1}^{M}g_{N}(p)\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi_{N}(Z_{i})}.

Since
\sqrt{N}\left(\frac{N_{p}}{N}-\pi_{p}\right)\to 0
,
p=1,\ldots,M
, the difference between

\frac{\frac{N_{p}}{N}f_{p}(z)}{\phi_{N}(z)}
and
\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(z)}{\phi(z)}
, and the difference between

g_{N}(z):=2\sum_{p=1}^{M}\frac{N_{p}}{N}K(z,p)-\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}\frac{N_{p}N_{q}}{N^{2}}K(p,q)

and

g(z):=2\sum_{p=1}^{M}\pi_{p}K(z,p)-\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}\pi_{p}\pi_{q}K(p,q)

are
o(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}})
, uniformly in
z
.
Hence the above statistic has the same limit in probability as:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi(Z_{i})}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(Z_{j})}{\phi(Z_{j})}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(Z_{i})}{\phi(Z_{i})},

We can write the above statistic as
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})
, where

\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}):=\frac{1}{d_{x}}\sum_{y:(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(y)}{\phi(y)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}.

(C.14)

From the radius of stabilization for
\mathcal{G}
, we also have the same
radius of stabilization

[
55
,
54
]

R=R(N,x)
for
\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})
for which the points outside
B(x,N^{-1/d}R)
cannot impact the value of
\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})
, i.e.,

\xi\left(x,[\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}\cap B(x,N^{-1/d}R)]\cup\mathcal{X}\right)=\xi\left(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}\cap B(x,N^{-1/d}R)\right),

(C.15)

for all finite
\mathcal{X}\subset A\backslash B(x,N^{-1/d}R)
.

The power-law stabilization of a function
\xi:(x,\mathcal{X})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a finite set, can be defined in a similar fashion as the power-law stabilization of a graph (see (
B.3
)):

Definition C.2

(Power-law stabilizing functions
[
55
]
)
.

Let
\xi(x,\mathcal{X})
be a real-valued function defined for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any finite set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
R\equiv R(N,x)
is a radius of stabilization as defined in (
C.15
). Let

\tau(t):=\sup_{N\geq 1,x\in A}\mathbb{P}[R(N,x)>t],\qquad\mbox{for}\;\;t>0.

Then,
\xi
is said to be:

1.

power-law stabilizing of order
q
with respect to
\phi_{N}
if
\sup_{t\geq 1}t^{q}\tau(t)<\infty
,

2.

exponentially stabilizing with respect to
\phi_{N}
if
\limsup_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\log\tau(t)<0
.

Let

T:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})

with
\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})
defined in (
C.14
).
The goal is to show the variance of
T
converges. By Palm theory,

\mathbb{E}[T]=\sqrt{N}\int\mathbb{E}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\phi(x){\rm d}x,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[T^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}\xi^{2}(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\right)+\frac{2}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\right]

\displaystyle=\int\mathbb{E}\xi^{2}(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\phi_{N}(x){\rm d}x+N\int\mathbb{E}\left[\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y})\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})\right]\phi_{N}(x)\phi_{N}(y){\rm d}x{\rm d}y

Hence,

\displaystyle{\rm Var}[T]

\displaystyle=\int\mathbb{E}\xi^{2}(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\phi_{N}(x){\rm d}x

(C.16)

\displaystyle+N\int\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y})\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})\right]-\mathbb{E}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\mathbb{E}\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\right)\phi_{N}(x)\phi_{N}(y){\rm d}x{\rm d}y.

The convergence of the first term above is easy to see.
Similar to previous arguments, by the coupling lemma
C.1
and the
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}
stabilization assumption,
(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{z}))
implies that
y
is a neighbor of
x
, and lies within
O_{p}\left(N^{-1/d}\right)
distance to
x
.
If
x
is a continuity point of
f_{q}
, for
q=1,\ldots,M
, then together with boundedness of

\xi^{2}(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})=\left(\frac{1}{d_{x}}\sum_{y:(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(y)}{\phi(y)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\right)^{2},

we can
replace
f_{q}(y)
and
\phi(y)
in the expression above by
f_{q}(x)
and
\phi(x)
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\xi^{2}(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{d_{x}}\sum_{y:(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(y)}{\phi(y)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{d_{x}}\sum_{y:(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(x)}{\phi(x)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(x)}{\phi(x)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\right)^{2}.

To show the convergence of the second term in (
C.16
), we first perform a change of variable
y=x+N^{-1/d}z
:

\displaystyle N\int\left(\mathbb{E}\Big[\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{y})\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})\right]-\mathbb{E}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\mathbb{E}\xi(y,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\Big)\phi_{N}(x)\phi_{N}(y){\rm d}x{\rm d}y=

(C.17)

\displaystyle\int\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\underbrace{\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x+N^{-\frac{1}{d}}z})}_{{\rm denoted\ by\ }X}\underbrace{\xi(x+N^{-\frac{1}{d}}z,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})}_{Z}\big]-\mathbb{E}\underbrace{\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})}_{X^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\underbrace{\xi(x+N^{-\frac{1}{d}}z,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})}_{Z^{\prime}}\Big)\phi_{N}(x)\phi_{N}(x+N^{-\frac{1}{d}}z){\rm d}x{\rm d}z.

When taking the limit as
N\to\infty
, we want to pass the limit inside the integral. We will show that the integrand is small for large
|z|
, uniformly in
N
. More specifically, there exists
C_{1}>0
free of
N,x,z
such that

|\mathbb{E}[XZ]-\mathbb{E}[X^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}]|\leq C_{1}(|z|^{-d-1/C_{1}}\wedge 1),

(C.18)

for all
N\geq 1,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
X,Z,X^{\prime},Z^{\prime}
are defined in (
C.17
).
Let
\tilde{X}:=X1_{R(x,N)\leq|z|/3}
,
\tilde{Z}:=Z1_{R(x+N^{-1/d}z,N)\leq|z|/3}
.
Then
\tilde{X}
and
\tilde{Z}
are independent because they are determined by the points of
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}

in
B(x,N^{-\frac{1}{d}}|z|/3)
,
B(x+N^{-\frac{1}{d}}z,N^{-\frac{1}{d}}|z|/3)
respectively.
So
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}\tilde{Z}]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}]\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}]
, and

\mathbb{E}[XZ]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}]\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}]+\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}(Z-\tilde{Z})]+\mathbb{E}[(X-\tilde{X})Z]

while

\mathbb{E}[X^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}]\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}]+\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}]\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}-\tilde{Z}]+\mathbb{E}[X^{\prime}-\tilde{X}]\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}].

Note that the absolute values of
X,Z,\tilde{X},\tilde{Z},X^{\prime},Z^{\prime}
are all bounded by a constant
C>0
depending only on the kernel
K
,
\pi
, and
\sup_{n}\{\frac{t_{n}}{r_{n}}\}
. Hence

|\mathbb{E}[(X-\tilde{X})Z]|\leq C\mathbb{E}|X-\tilde{X}|=C\mathbb{E}|X|1_{R(x,N)>|z|/3}\leq C^{2}\mathbb{P}(R(x,N)>|z|/3).

If we have power-law stabilization of order
q>d
, then there exists
C_{2}>0
such that
C^{2}\mathbb{P}(R(x,N)>|z|/3)\leq C_{2}(|z|^{-d+1/C_{2}}\wedge 1)
. Similarly,
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}(Z-\tilde{Z})]
,
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}]\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}-\tilde{Z}]
, and
\mathbb{E}[(X-\tilde{X})Z]
are all controlled by
C_{2}(|z|^{-d+1/C_{2}}\wedge 1)
. Hence (
C.18
) is proved.
This implies that the contribution of
|z|>K
to the integral in (
C.17
) is sufficiently small for large
K
, and hence we can pass the limit into the integral by dominated convergence. It remains to compute the limit of the integrand:

\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x+N^{-\frac{1}{d}}z})\xi(x+N^{-\frac{1}{d}}z,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})\big]-\mathbb{E}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\mathbb{E}\xi(x+N^{-\frac{1}{d}}z,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\Big)\phi_{N}(x)\phi_{N}(x+N^{-\frac{1}{d}}z)

as
N\to\infty
.
We show each term above converges. Suppose
x
is a continuity point of
f_{p}
,
p=1,\ldots,M
.
With the same coupling technique used previously, we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d_{x}}\sum_{y:(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(y)}{\phi(y)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d_{x}}\sum_{y:(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(x)}{\phi(x)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(x)}{\phi(x)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}.

Lemma
C.1
shows that
N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}-x)
can be locally approximated by
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x)}
,
and thus
N^{1/d}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x+N^{-1/d}z}-x)
can be approximated by
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x)}\cup\{z\}
.
Since the graph is translation invariant and stabilizing on
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x)}^{\mathbf{0}}
, it is also stabilizing on
\mathcal{P}_{\phi(x)}^{z}

[
56
, Lemma 3.3]
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\xi(x+N^{-1/d}z,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\frac{1}{d_{x+N^{-1/d}z}}\sum_{y:(x+N^{-1/d}z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x+N^{-1/d}z})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x+N^{-1/d}z)}{\phi(x+N^{-1/d}z)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(y)}{\phi(y)}

\displaystyle-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x+N^{-1/d}z)}{\phi(x+N^{-1/d}z)}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{d_{x+N^{-1/d}z}}\sum_{y:(x+N^{-1/d}z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x+N^{-1/d}z})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(x)}{\phi(x)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(x)}{\phi(x)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}.

Further, as shown in the proof of
Penrose
et al. 2003
, if a geometric graph is translation invariant and stabilizing on
\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\mathbf{0}}
, then it is also stabilizing on
\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\{\mathbf{0},z\}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x+N^{-1/d}z})\xi(x+N^{-1/d}z,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x})\big]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Big(\frac{1}{d_{x}}\sum_{y:(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,x+N^{-1/d}z})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(x)}{\phi(x)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\Big)\times

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{d_{x+N^{-1/d}z}}\sum_{y:(x+N^{-1/d}z,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}^{x,x+N^{-1/d}z})}\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(x)}{\phi(x)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(x)}{\phi(x)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\right)^{2}.

Hence the integrand in (
C.17
) converges pointwise to 0. Combining the above convergence results we have:

{\rm Var}[T]\to\int\left(\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\frac{\pi_{q}f_{q}(x)}{\phi(x)}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\frac{\pi_{p}f_{p}(x)}{\phi(x)}\right)^{2}\phi(x){\rm d}x=:\kappa_{2}^{2}.

If
\kappa_{2}^{2}=0
, then
T-\mathbb{E}T\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{2}^{2})
is trivial. Suppose
\kappa_{2}^{2}>0
. We will use the following proposition.

Proposition C.1
.

Suppose that
\xi(x,\mathcal{X})
is a bounded function, defined for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any finite set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which is power-law stabilizing with respect to
\phi_{N}
with order
q_{0}>16d
(see Definition
C.2
).
Suppose
\phi(\cdot)
is bounded and has bounded support.
Let
T=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}}\xi(x,\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})
.
Suppose
{\rm Var}(T)\to\sigma^{2}>0
. Then
T-\mathbb{E}T\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\sigma^{2})
.

Proof.

We first cover
{\rm supp}(\phi)
by
V=V(N)
cubes of the form
Q=\prod_{i=1}^{d}[j_{i}s_{N},(j_{i}+1)s_{N}]
,
where
s_{N}:=N^{-1/d}\rho_{N}
. Here
\rho_{N}
will be taken as
N^{a}
such that

a<\frac{1}{8d}
and
(aq_{0}-1)>1
.
Since
\phi
has bounded support,
V(N)=O\left(\frac{1}{s_{N}^{d}}\right)=O\left(N\rho_{N}^{-d}\right)
.
The points in
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
can be labelled as:

X_{i,j},\ 1\leq i\leq V,\ 1\leq j\leq M_{i},\ M_{i}\sim{\rm Poisson}\left(N\int_{Q_{i}}\phi_{N}(x){\rm d}x\right)

where
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{V}
is an enumeration of the cubes that cover
{\rm supp}(\phi)
as described above. As
\phi
is bounded, each
f_{i}\ (i=1,\ldots,M)
is also bounded, and consequently
\|\phi_{N}\|_{\infty}
is bounded by a constant for all
N
, and
N\int_{Q_{i}}\phi_{N}(x){\rm d}x\leq N\|\phi_{N}\|_{\infty}\int_{Q_{i}}{\rm d}x=Ns_{N}^{d}\|\phi_{N}\|_{\infty}=\rho_{N}^{d}\|\phi_{N}\|_{\infty}
. If we write
\xi_{i,j}:=\xi(X_{i,j},\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})
, then we have the following bound on the
q
-th moment (i.e., for any random variable
X
,
\|X\|_{q}:=(\mathbb{E}|X|^{q})^{1/q}
):

\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{M_{i}}|\xi_{i,j}|\right\|_{q}\leq\|\xi\|_{\infty}\|M_{i}\|_{q}\leq\|\xi\|_{\infty}\left\|{\rm Poisson}\left(\rho_{N}^{d}\|\phi_{N}\|_{\infty}\right)\right\|_{q}.

Using the fact that
\|{\rm Poisson}(\lambda)\|_{q}\leq\lambda\exp(q/(2\lambda))
(see
[
1
]
),
and
\|\phi_{N}\|_{\infty}
being bounded,
we have:

\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{M_{i}}|\xi_{i,j}|\right\|_{q}\lesssim\rho_{N}^{d}.

(C.19)

Let
R_{i,j}
denote the radius of stabilization of
\xi
at
X_{i,j}
,
E_{i,j}:=\{R_{i,j}\leq\rho_{N}\}
,
E:=\cap_{i=1}^{V}\cap_{j=1}^{M_{i}}E_{i.j}
. Then by Palm theory
[
53
, Theorem 1.6]
,

\mathbb{P}[E^{c}]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{V}\sum_{j=1}^{M_{i}}1_{E_{i,j}^{c}}\right]=N\int\mathbb{P}[R(x,N)>\rho_{N}]\phi_{N}(x){\rm d}x\leq N\tau(\rho_{N}).

Since
\xi
is power law stabilizing of order
q_{0}
,
\tau(t)\leq\frac{C}{t^{q_{0}}}
for some
C>0
.
Since
\rho_{N}=N^{a}
, we have

\mathbb{P}[E^{c}]\leq\frac{C}{N^{aq_{0}-1}}
.
Consider

T^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{V(N)}\sum_{j=1}^{M_{i}}\xi(X_{i,j},\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\cdot 1_{E_{i,j}}

which equals
T
on
E
, and is a sum of
V(N)
“near independent” random variables. We show that
{\rm Var}[T^{\prime}]
is close to
{\rm Var}[T]
. By Hölder’s inequality, for
q>2
:

\displaystyle\|T-T^{\prime}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|T-T^{\prime}\|_{q}\mathbb{P}[E^{c}]^{1-\frac{2}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\|T\|_{q}+\|T^{\prime}\|_{q}\right)\left(\frac{C}{N^{aq_{0}-1}}\right)^{1-\frac{2}{q}}.

To bound
\|T\|_{q}
and
\|T^{\prime}\|_{q}
, we use (
C.19
) and
V(N)=O\left(N\rho_{N}^{-d}\right)
to get, for
q\geq 2
,

\|T^{\prime}\|_{q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{V(N)}\sum_{j=1}^{M_{i}}\xi(X_{i,j},\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\cdot 1_{E_{i,j}}\right\|_{q}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\cdot N\rho_{N}^{-d}\cdot\rho_{N}^{d}=\sqrt{N}.

Similarly
\|T\|_{q}\lesssim\sqrt{N}
. Hence

\|T-T^{\prime}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{N}\cdot\left(\frac{C}{N^{aq_{0}-1}}\right)^{1-\frac{2}{q}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle|{\rm Var}(T)-{\rm Var}(T^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=|{\rm Var}(T-T^{\prime})+2{\rm Cov}(T-T^{\prime},T^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\|T-T^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}+2\|T-T^{\prime}\|_{2}\|T^{\prime}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim N\left(\frac{C}{N^{ap-1}}\right)^{2-\frac{4}{q}}+2\cdot\sqrt{N}\left(\frac{C}{N^{ap-1}}\right)^{1-\frac{2}{q}}\cdot\sqrt{N}.

We can choose
q
large enough such that
(aq_{0}-1)(1-2/q)-1>0
. Then we have
|{\rm Var}(T)-{\rm Var}(T^{\prime})|\to 0
.
In particular,
{\rm Var}(T^{\prime})\to\sigma^{2}>0
. Also,
|\mathbb{E}T-\mathbb{E}T^{\prime}|\leq\mathbb{E}|T-T^{\prime}|\leq\|T-T^{\prime}\|_{2}\to 0
.

Write
\left(T^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}[T^{\prime}]\right)/\sqrt{{\rm Var}(T^{\prime})}=\sum_{i=1}^{V}S_{i}
, where

S_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N{\rm Var}(T^{\prime})}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{M_{i}}\xi(X_{i,j},\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\cdot 1_{E_{i,j}}-\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{M_{i}}\xi(X_{i,j},\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}})\cdot 1_{E_{i,j}}\right]\right).

Construct a dependency graph on
\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{V}\}
as follows: for
i\neq j
, there is an edge between
S_{i}
and
S_{j}
if
d(Q_{i},Q_{j})\leq 2N^{-1/d}\rho_{N}
, where
d(Q_{i},Q_{j}):=\inf\{|x-y|:x\in Q_{i},y\in Q_{j}\}
.
By definition of the radius of stabilization
R(x,N)
, the value of
S_{i}
is determined by the restriction of
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
to the
N^{-1/d}\rho_{N}
-neighborhood of the cube
Q_{i}
, i.e.,
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|x-y|\leq N^{-1/d}\rho_{N}\textrm{ for some }y\in Q_{i}\}
.
By the independence property of the Poisson process,
for any pair of disjoint sets
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\subset\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{V}\}
such that no edge has one endpoint in
\Gamma_{1}
and the other in
\Gamma_{2}
,
\Gamma_{1}
is independent of
\Gamma_{2}
. Since the number of cubes in
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{V}
that are at most
2N^{-1/d}\rho_{N}
distant from a given cube is bounded by
5^{d}
, it follows that the maximal degree of the dependency graph is bounded by
D\leq 5^{d}
, a constant.

By (
C.19
) and
{\rm Var}(T^{\prime})\to\sigma^{2}>0
,

\mathbb{E}[|S_{i}|^{3}]\lesssim\frac{1}{N^{3/2}}\rho_{N}^{3d}=\frac{1}{N^{3(0.5-ad)}}.

Hence we can take
p=3
and
\theta=\frac{C^{\prime}}{N^{0.5-ad}}
for some constant
C^{\prime}
in Theorem
C.3
to yield

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{T^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}[T^{\prime}]}{\sqrt{{\rm Var}(T^{\prime})}}\leq z\right)-\Phi(z)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 75D^{5(p-1)}|\mathcal{V}|\theta^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim N\rho_{N}^{-d}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{3(0.5-ad)}}=\frac{1}{N^{0.5-4ad}}\to 0,

as
N\to\infty
, which implies
\frac{T^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}[T^{\prime}]}{\sqrt{{\rm Var}(T^{\prime})}}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,1)
.
Since
|{\rm Var}(T)-{\rm Var}(T^{\prime})|\to 0
,
|\mathbb{E}T-\mathbb{E}T^{\prime}|\to 0
, and
T,T^{\prime}
coincide on the set
E
whose probability converges to 1, we have
\frac{T-\mathbb{E}[T]}{\sqrt{{\rm Var}(T)}}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,1)
.
∎

C.13
Proof of Theorem
B.2

Note that Lemmas
C.1
and
C.3
still hold if
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}}
is replaced by
\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}^{N}}
and
\phi(x_{0})
is replaced by
f(x_{0})
, assuming
f_{1}^{N},\ldots,f_{M}^{N}
are equicontinuous at
x_{0}
and
f_{i}^{N}(x_{0})\to f(x_{0})
as
N\to\infty
.
The slight change in the proof is that (
C.9
) now becomes:

N\int_{B(x_{0},N^{-1/d}K)}|\phi_{N}^{N}(x)-f(x_{0})|{\rm d}x\leq N\int_{B(x_{0},N^{-1/d}K)}(|\phi_{N}^{N}(x)-\phi_{N}^{N}(x_{0})|+|\phi_{N}^{N}(x_{0})-f(x_{0})|){\rm d}x\to 0,

since
\phi_{N}^{N}
is equicontinuous at
x_{0}
and
\phi_{N}^{N}(x_{0})\to f(x_{0})
.
The rest of the proof follows almost verbatim from the proof of Theorem
B.1
,
by replacing every
f_{i}
by
f_{i}^{N}
, every
\phi_{N}
by
\phi_{N}^{N}
, every
\phi
by
\phi^{N}:=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}f_{i}^{N}
, and applying an extra limit (as
N\to\infty
):

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\int|\phi^{N}(z)-f(z)|{\rm d}z\to 0,\\
&\frac{\pi_{i}f_{i}^{N}(z)}{\phi^{N}(z)}\to\pi_{i},\qquad\mbox{for a.e.}\;z\in{\rm supp}(f),\qquad\mbox{for }\;i=1,\ldots,M.\end{aligned}\right.

The limiting variance
\kappa_{1}^{2}
reduces to the numerator of the asymptotic null variance in (
8
), which is distribution-free. The limiting variance

\kappa_{2}^{2}=\int\left(\sum_{p,q=1}^{M}K(p,q)\pi_{p}\pi_{q}-\sum_{p=1}^{M}g(p)\pi_{p}\right)^{2}f(x){\rm d}x=0.

Recall that
g(\Delta)=2\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pi_{i}K(\Delta,i)-\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}K(i,j)
was defined in Theorem
B.1
.
Hence,
\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})-\mathbb{E}(H_{n})\overset{d}{\to}N(0,\kappa_{2}^{2})\equiv\delta_{0}
as the variance of
\mathbb{E}(H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n})
converges to 0. Note that we need
q_{0}>d
here to establish the convergence of
{\rm Var}\left(\mathbb{E}[H_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{n}]\right)
; see (
C.18
). Recall that the power-law stabilization with order
q_{0}>16d
was used for proving Proposition
C.1
which establishes the CLT assuming the variance converges to a non-zero quantity, and is not needed in this proof.

C.14
Proof of Theorem
B.3

Since

\frac{n\tilde{H}_{n}-\mathbb{E}[n\tilde{H}_{n}]}{\sqrt{n}\kappa_{1,{\rm null}}}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,1),

the local power of the test in (
B.7
) is

\Phi\left(z_{\alpha}+\lim\frac{\mathbb{E}[n\tilde{H}_{n}]}{\kappa_{1,{\rm null}}\sqrt{n}}\right).

As
\frac{n}{N}\overset{p}{\to}1
, we know that
\frac{\mathbb{E}[n\tilde{H}_{n}]}{\sqrt{n}}
has the same limit in probability as the expectation of

\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{N}(\Delta_{i}).

Note that the expectation of the second term
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{N}(\Delta_{i})
equals the expectation of the first term
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum\limits_{j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}K(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})
under the null, i.e.,
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}
. Hence we study the first term as follows. With a
k
-NN graph and the discrete kernel, the expectation of the first term can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k\sqrt{N}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i,j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}\left(1-I(\Delta_{i}\neq\Delta_{j})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{N}-\frac{1}{k\sqrt{N}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i,j:(Z_{i},Z_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}I(\Delta_{i}\neq\Delta_{j})\right]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{N}-\frac{1}{k\sqrt{N}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x,y:(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}^{N}})}\left(\frac{\frac{N_{1}}{N}f(x|\theta_{1})}{\phi_{N}^{N}(x)}\frac{\frac{N_{2}}{N}f(y|\theta_{2})}{\phi_{N}^{N}(y)}+\frac{\frac{N_{2}}{N}f(x|\theta_{2})}{\phi_{N}^{N}(x)}\frac{\frac{N_{1}}{N}f(y|\theta_{1})}{\phi_{N}^{N}(y)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{N}-\frac{1}{k\sqrt{N}}N_{1}N_{2}\int_{S\times S}(f(x|\theta_{1})f(y|\theta_{2})+f(x|\theta_{2})f(y|\theta_{1}))\rho_{K}^{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}(x,y){\rm d}x{\rm d}y,

where
\phi_{N}^{N}(x)=\frac{N_{1}}{N}f(x|\theta_{1})+\frac{N_{2}}{N}f(x|\theta_{2})
,
and
\rho_{K}^{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}(x,y)=\mathbb{P}\left((x,y)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{N\phi_{N}^{N}}^{x,y})\right)
.
The last equality above follows from Palm theory.
Define

\mu_{N}^{(S)}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}):=N^{2}\int_{S\times S}(f(x|\theta_{1})f(y|\theta_{2})+f(x|\theta_{2})f(y|\theta_{1}))\rho_{K}^{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}(x,y){\rm d}x{\rm d}y.

Then,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}[n\tilde{H}_{n}]}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}-\frac{N_{1}N_{2}}{kN^{2}}\frac{\mu_{N}^{(S)}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})-\mu_{N}^{(S)}(\theta_{1},\theta_{1})}{\sqrt{N}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}-\frac{N_{1}N_{2}}{kN^{2}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{N}^{\top}\nabla\mu_{N}^{(S)}(\theta_{1},\theta_{1})}{\sqrt{N}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\varepsilon_{N}^{\top}{\rm H}\mu_{N}^{(S)}(\theta_{1},\theta_{1})\varepsilon_{N}}{\sqrt{N}}+\mathcal{R}_{N}\right),

where the gradient and Hessian are taken with respect to
\theta_{2}
.
The limit of the gradient and Hessian follows the argument in Appendix E in the supplementary file of
Bhattacharya 2020
.
Note that there is a typo in the limit of the Hessian term in
Bhattacharya 2020
. In fact, with
\varepsilon_{N}=hN^{-1/4}
, we can show that

\frac{\varepsilon_{N}^{\top}{\rm H}\mu_{N}^{(S)}(\theta_{1},\theta_{1})\varepsilon_{N}}{\sqrt{N}}\to-2rk\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{h^{\top}\nabla_{\theta_{1}}f(X|\theta_{1})}{f(X|\theta_{1})}\right]^{2},

where
r=2\pi_{1}\pi_{2}
. The coefficient above is 2 instead of
3/2
in the original version of
Bhattacharya 2020
.
The rest of the proof follows from the arguments at the beginning of
Bhattacharya 2020
.
∎

C.15
Proof of Theorem
C.1

The proof is similar to that in
Pham
et al. 1989
.
But for completeness, we provide the entire proof here. The proof applies the method of moments, an idea that dates back to Pafnutii Lvovich Chebyshev (1821–1894)
[
27
]
. More specifically, we will show that for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\sum a_{ij}B_{ij}}{{\rm Var}(\sum a_{ij}B_{ij})}\right)^{r}\right]\to\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{r}\right],

where
Z\sim N(0,1)
. This implies that for any subsequence that converges in distribution, the limiting distribution has the same moments as
N(0,1)
,
and therefore must be
N(0,1)
as these moments uniquely determine the distribution
N(0,1)
(see e.g.,
[
12
, Theorem 30.1]
).
Since weak convergence to
N(0,1)
holds for any subsequence, it further implies that the entire sequence converges in distribution as weak convergence is metrizable.

In this proof, we will decompose
\left(\sum a_{ij}B_{ij}\right)^{r}
into “sums corresponding to different graphs” as in
Bloemena 1964
. The sums corresponding to most of the graphs will be negligible when compared to
\left[{\rm Var}(\sum a_{ij}B_{ij})\right]^{r}
. The remaining dominating terms will lead to the moments of
N(0,1)
.

We first define equivalent graphs. In this proof, a graph will be considered as a collection of edges (so it does not contain isolated vertices), and we will consider the edges in a graph to be different, each having a label (from
1,\ldots,r
) and a direction.
Two graphs are equivalent if they can be mapped to each other while keeping the direction and the labeling of the edges.
Multiple edges are allowed to exist between 2 vertices. For a graph
G
, denote by
|G|
the number of its vertices, and arbitrarily label the vertices of
G
as
1,\ldots,|G|
.
Then an edge in
G
can be given by
(\mu,\nu)
, where
\mu,\nu\in\{1,\ldots,|G|\}
are integers. Define

\Sigma(a,G):={{\sum}^{\prime}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}}}\prod_{(\mu,\nu)\in G}a_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}},

(C.20)

where
\sum^{\prime}
means the summation is over distinct
i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Denote by
G(r)
the set of all graphs with
r
edges (equivalent graphs will only be counted once). Then

(\sum a_{ij})^{r}=\sum_{G\in G(r)}\Sigma(a,G).

(C.21)

It can be seen from (
C.20
) that if
G
has a self-loop, then
\Sigma(a,G)=0
as
a_{ii}=b_{ii}=0
,
\forall i
.
Hence, we can ignore graphs in
G(r)
that contain self-loops.
To understand (
C.21
),
for example, when
r=2
,

\displaystyle\left(\sum a_{ij}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle={\sum}^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{kl}

\displaystyle+{\sum}^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{ik}+{\sum}^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{ki}+{\sum}^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{jk}+{\sum}^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{kj}

\displaystyle+{\sum}^{\prime}a_{ij}^{2}+{\sum}^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{ji}.

The sum in the first line corresponds to a graph with two isolated edges.
The sums in the second line correspond to graphs with two edges sharing exactly one vertex, but the edges can have different orientations.
The sums in the third line correspond to graphs with two edges sharing two vertices (which forms a cycle), with two possible orientations.

Now,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sum a_{ij}B_{ij}\right)^{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{G\in G(r)}\dsum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}}\mathbb{E}\prod_{(\mu,\nu)\in G}a_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}B_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}

(C.22)

\displaystyle=\sum_{G\in G(r)}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{(\mu,\nu)\in G}B_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}\right]\Sigma(a,G)

\displaystyle=\sum_{G\in G(r)}\frac{1}{n(n-1)\cdots(n-|G|+1)}\Sigma(b,G)\Sigma(a,G).

The second equality follows from the fact that each term
\prod_{(\mu,\nu)\in G}B_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}
has the same expectation, which is
\frac{1}{n(n-1)\cdots(n-|G|+1)}\Sigma(b,G)
as there are
n(n-1)\cdots(n-|G|+1)
many terms in
\Sigma(b,G)
.

We first estimate the order of
\Sigma(b,G)
. Suppose
G
has an isolated edge, say
(1,2)
.
By definition,
\Sigma(b,G)={{\sum}^{\prime}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}}}\prod_{(\mu,\nu)\in G}b_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}
with the summation indices
i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}
required to be distinct.
If we relax the constraint for
i_{1},i_{2}
, i.e.,
i_{1},i_{2}
can freely take values from
1,\ldots,n
while
i_{3},\ldots,i_{|G|}
are still required to be distinct, then the sum will be 0 as
\sum b_{ij}=0
. But then we need to subtract back the terms which were originally not in
\Sigma(b,G)
and came in because of relaxing the constraints on
i_{1},i_{2}
. These terms are sum of a number of
\Sigma(b,G^{\prime})
, where
G^{\prime}
is the graph obtained from
G
by identifying vertex 1 or 2 or both with some vertex in
3,\ldots,|G|
.
In general, if
G
has multiple isolated edges,
\Sigma(b,G)
can be written as a linear combination of
\Sigma(b,G^{\prime})
, where
G^{\prime}
can be obtained by sequentially identifying the vertices of isolated edges with other vertices in the graph, and
G^{\prime}
no longer has any isolated edge. Note that

|\Sigma(b,G^{\prime})|=\left|\dsum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G^{\prime}|}}\prod_{(\mu,\nu)\in G^{\prime}}b_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}\right|\leq\prod_{(\mu,\nu)\in G^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G^{\prime}|}}|b_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}|^{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}},

where we have used the fact that for
C_{ij}\geq 0
,
1\leq i\leq I
,
1\leq j\leq J
,

\prod_{i=1}^{I}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}C_{ij}^{r}\right)\geq\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\prod_{i=1}^{I}C_{ij}\right)^{r}.

Since
\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G^{\prime}|}}|b_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}|^{r}=n^{|G^{\prime}|-2}\sum|b_{ij}|^{r}
and by (B2), it is
O\left(n^{|G^{\prime}|-r}\|b\|^{r}\right)
,
where
\|b\|
is defined as
(\sum b_{ij}^{2})^{1/2}
,
we have
|\Sigma(b,G^{\prime})|=O\left(n^{|G^{\prime}|-r}\|b\|^{r}\right)
.

Let
s
be the number of isolated edges in
G
. Note that if
(1,2)
is an isolated edge, identifying
1
or
2
with some vertex in
3,\ldots,|G|
reduces the number of vertices by 1, and the number of isolated edges by at most 2. Identifying both
1
and
2
with some vertices in
3,\ldots,|G|
reduces the number of vertices by 2, and the number of isolated edges by at most 3. Hence in either way, when the number of vertices is reduced by 1, the number of isolated edges is reduced by at most 2. Since
G^{\prime}
has no isolated edge,
|G^{\prime}|\leq|G|-\lceil\frac{s}{2}\rceil
.
Combining the discussions above:

\Sigma(b,G)=O\left(n^{|G|-\lceil\frac{s}{2}\rceil-r}\cdot\|b\|^{r}\right).

(C.23)

Next, we estimate the order of
\Sigma(a,G)
. Without loss of generality, we can suppose
\max|a_{ij}|=1
.
Then, if
G
is connected, by suppressing redundant edges in
G
to a spanning tree
T
, we have:

\Sigma(|a|,G):=\dsum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}}\prod_{(\mu,\nu)\in G}|a_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}|\leq\dsum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}}\prod_{(\mu,\nu)\in T}|a_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}|.

Here
|a|
means taking the absolute value of each
a_{ij}
.
Recall assumption (A1):
\max_{i}\sum_{j}|a_{ij}|=O(\max|a_{ij}|\cdot t_{n})
.
Hence by sequentially summing over the index corresponding to a leaf,

\Sigma(|a|,G)\leq\dsum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}}\prod_{(\mu,\nu)\in T}|a_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}|\leq nt_{n}^{|G|-1}.

If
G
has
c
connected components,
we can relax the summation constraint “
i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}
are distinct” to “the indices within each component are distinct”, which implies

\Sigma(|a|,G)\leq n^{c}t_{n}^{|G|-c}\leq n^{c}t_{n}^{|G|}.

If among these
c
connected components,
s
of them are isolated edges,
then similar to the previous argument, using
\sum a_{ij}=0
,
\Sigma(a,G)
can be written
as a linear combination of
\Sigma(a,G^{\prime})
, where
G^{\prime}
can be obtained by sequentially identifying the vertices of isolated edges with other vertices in the graph, and
G^{\prime}
no longer has any isolated edge.
Note that if
(1,2)
is an isolated edge, identifying
1
or
2
with some vertex in
3,\ldots,|G|

reduces the number of connected components by 1 and the number of isolated edges by 1 or 2. Identifying both
1
and
2
with some vertices in
3,\ldots,|G|
may reduce the number of isolated edges by 3, but in such a case the number of connected components is reduced by 2. Hence in either case, when the number of connected components is reduced by 1, the number of isolated edges is reduced by at most 2.
Because
G^{\prime}
no longer has any isolated edge, it has at most
c-\lceil\frac{s}{2}\rceil
connected components.
Therefore,

\Sigma(a,G)=O\left(n^{c-\lceil\frac{s}{2}\rceil}t_{n}^{|G|}\right).

Recall assumption (A2):
\liminf\sum a_{ij}^{2}/(n\max a_{ij}^{2})>0
, which implies

\Sigma(a,G)=O\left(n^{c-\lceil\frac{s}{2}\rceil-\frac{r}{2}}t_{n}^{|G|}\cdot\|a\|^{r}\right).

(C.24)

By considering the number of edges:
2(c-s)+s\leq r\Leftrightarrow c\leq\frac{r}{2}+\frac{s}{2}
,
we know

\Sigma(a,G)\leq n^{-1/2}t_{n}^{|G|}\cdot\|a\|^{r}=o\left(\|a\|^{r}\right),

unless every connected component has at most 2 edges and
\lceil\frac{s}{2}\rceil=\frac{s}{2}
.
Together with
\Sigma(b,G)=O\left(n^{|G|-\lceil\frac{s}{2}\rceil-r}\cdot\|b\|^{r}\right)
deducted previously, we have

\frac{1}{n(n-1)\cdots(n-|G|+1)}\Sigma(b,G)\Sigma(a,G)=o\left(\frac{\|a\|^{r}\|b\|^{r}}{n^{r}}\right),

unless
s=0
and every connected component has at most 2 edges, which is then equivalent to all connected components having exactly 2 edges.

We now claim that
w_{n}\gtrsim\frac{\|a\|\|b\|}{n}
. Hence in the expansion (
C.22
) of
\mathbb{E}\left(\sum a_{ij}B_{ij}\right)^{r}
, the sum corresponding to these graphs are negligible compared to
w_{n}^{r}
.
To see the claim, note that
\sum a_{ij}=0
implies
\sum^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{ik}=\sum a_{i+}^{2}-\sum a_{ij}^{2}
, and similarly
\sum^{\prime}b_{ij}b_{ik}=\sum b_{i+}^{2}-\sum b_{ij}^{2}
. Hence,

\displaystyle w_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4(n-2)}{n^{4}}\left(\sum a_{i+}^{2}\right)\left(\sum b_{i+}^{2}\right)

(C.25)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\left(\sum a_{ij}^{2}-\frac{2}{n}\sum a_{i+}^{2}\right)\left(\sum b_{ij}^{2}-\frac{2}{n}\sum b_{i+}^{2}\right)+\frac{4}{n^{3}}\left(\sum a_{ij}^{2}\right)\left(\sum b_{ij}^{2}\right).

From (A1), (A2), and (B1), we know that the second term in the right-hand side of (
C.25
) is
\gtrsim\frac{\|a\|^{2}\|b\|^{2}}{n^{2}}
.
Hence the third term in (
C.25
) is negligible compared to the second term.
Moreover, since
{\rm Var}\left(\sum a_{ij}B_{ij}\right)
has the following expression
[
58
, Equation (2.6)]
:

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\left(\sum a_{ij}B_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{(n-1)(n-2)^{2}}\left(\sum a_{i+}^{2}\right)\left(\sum b_{i+}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{n(n-3)}\left(\sum a_{ij}^{2}-\frac{2n-5}{(n-2)^{2}}\sum a_{i+}^{2}\right)\left(\sum b_{ij}^{2}-\frac{2n-5}{(n-2)^{2}}\sum b_{i+}^{2}\right),

we have
w_{n}^{2}/{\rm Var}(\sum a_{ij}B_{ij})\to 1
.

Now consider the remaining graphs
G
consisting of connected components
G_{1},\ldots,G_{p}
, each of which has exactly 2 edges.
Hence necessarily,
r=2p
is even in order that
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\sum a_{ij}B_{ij}}{{\rm Var}(\sum a_{ij}B_{ij})}\right)^{r}\right]
has a non-zero limit.
Each
G_{q}
has two possible configurations
14
14

14

the configuration of a graph is the blank graph obtained by disregarding the direction of all edges
, either having 2 vertices and 2 edges forming a loop, or having 3 vertices and 2 edges forming a tree.
For the loop configuration,
\Sigma(a,G_{q})=\sum^{\prime}a_{ij}^{2}=\sum a_{ij}^{2}
.
For the tree configuration,
\Sigma(a,G_{q})=\sum^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{ik}=\sum a_{ij}a_{ik}
.
Since
\Sigma(a,G)=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}}^{\prime}\prod_{(\mu,\nu)\in G}a_{i_{\mu}i_{\nu}}
,
if we relax the constraint that
i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}
are distinct,
then we get
\Sigma(a,G_{1}\cup\cdots\cup G_{p})=\prod_{q=1}^{p}\Sigma(a,G_{q})+R_{1}
.
The difference
R_{1}
is the sum of a number of
\Sigma(a,G^{\prime})
, where
G^{\prime}
is obtained from identifying vertices of
G_{1}\cup\cdots\cup G_{p}
.
Since
G^{\prime}
has at most
p-1
connected components, by (
C.24
),
\Sigma(a,G^{\prime})=O\left(n^{p-1-\frac{r}{2}}t^{|G|}_{n}\cdot\|a\|^{r}\right)=O\left(\frac{t^{|G|}_{n}}{n}\cdot\|a\|^{r}\right)
.
Similarly, we can relax the constraint that
i_{1},\ldots,i_{|G|}
are distinct in
\Sigma(b,G_{1}\cup\cdots\cup G_{p})
,
which leads to
\Sigma(b,G)=\prod_{q=1}^{p}\Sigma(b,G_{q})+R_{2}
and the difference
R_{2}
is a sum of a number of
\Sigma(b,G^{\prime})
.
Since
|G^{\prime}|\leq|G|-1
, by (
C.23
),
\Sigma(b,G^{\prime})=O\left(n^{|G|-r-1}\cdot\|b\|^{r}\right)
.
Note that (
C.23
) and (
C.24
) also give

\Sigma(a,G)=O\left(t^{|G|}_{n}\cdot\|a\|^{r}\right)
,
\Sigma(b,G)=O\left(n^{|G|-r}\cdot\|b\|^{r}\right)
.
Hence together with
w_{n}\gtrsim\frac{\|a\|\cdot\|b\|}{n}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n(n-1)\cdots(n-|G|+1)}\Sigma(b,G)\Sigma(a,G)

\displaystyle=\frac{(n-|G|)!}{n!}\prod_{q=1}^{p}\Sigma(a,G_{q})\Sigma(b,G_{q})+o(w_{n}^{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{|G|}}\prod_{q=1}^{p}\Sigma(a,G_{q})\Sigma(b,G_{q})+o(w_{n}^{r}).

The second equality in the above display follows as
\frac{(n-|G|)!}{n!}-\frac{1}{n^{|G|}}=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{|G|+1}}\right)
and

\prod_{q=1}^{p}\Sigma(a,G_{q})\Sigma(b,G_{q})=O\left(n^{|G|-r}t^{|G|}_{n}\cdot\|a\|^{r}\|b\|^{r}\right)
.
Therefore, (
C.22
) reduces to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\sum a_{ij}B_{ij}}{w_{n}}\right)^{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{n}^{r}}\sum_{G\in\tilde{G}(r)}\frac{1}{n^{|G|}}\prod_{q=1}^{p}\Sigma(a,G_{q})\Sigma(b,G_{q})+o(1),

where
\tilde{G}(r)
contains the graphs that consists of connected components
G_{1},\ldots,G_{p}
, each of which has exactly 2 edges.

Suppose
i
of
G_{1},\ldots,G_{p}
have a loop configuration and
p-i
of them have a tree configuration. Then
|G|=2i+3(p-i)
and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{|G|}}\prod_{q=1}^{p}\Sigma(a,G_{q})\Sigma(b,G_{q})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{3p-i}}\left(\sum a_{ij}^{2}\right)^{i}\left(\sum b_{ij}^{2}\right)^{i}\left({\sum}^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{ik}\right)^{p-i}\left({\sum}^{\prime}b_{ij}b_{ik}\right)^{p-i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{3p-i}}\xi^{i}\eta^{p-i},

where
\xi:=\left(\sum a_{ij}^{2}\right)\left(\sum b_{ij}^{2}\right)
and
\eta:=\left({\sum}^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{ik}\right)\left({\sum}^{\prime}b_{ij}b_{ik}\right)
.

The number of graphs having
i
loops and
p-i
trees is given by:

\frac{\tbinom{2p}{2}\tbinom{2p-2}{2}\cdots\tbinom{2}{2}}{i!(p-i)!}\cdot 2^{i}\cdot 4^{p-i}=\frac{(2p)!2^{p-i}}{i!(p-i)!},

where we first divide the
2p
different edges into
i
loops and
p-i
trees, and then determine the orientation of each loop and tree.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{G\in\tilde{G}(r)}\frac{1}{n^{|G|}}\prod_{q=1}^{p}\Sigma(a,G_{q})\Sigma(b,G_{q})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{p}\frac{(2p)!2^{p-i}}{i!(p-i)!}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{3p-i}}\xi^{i}\eta^{p-i}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2p)!(2\eta)^{p}}{n^{3p}p!}\sum_{i=0}^{p}\frac{p!}{i!(p-i)!}\left(\frac{\xi}{2\eta n}\right)^{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2p)!(2\eta)^{p}}{n^{3p}p!}\left(1+\frac{\xi}{2\eta n}\right)^{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2p)!}{p!}\left(\frac{2\eta}{n^{3}}+\frac{\xi}{n^{4}}\right)^{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2p)!}{2^{p}p!}\left(\frac{4\left({\sum}^{\prime}a_{ij}a_{ik}\right)\left({\sum}^{\prime}b_{ij}b_{ik}\right)}{n^{3}}+\frac{2\left(\sum a_{ij}^{2}\right)\left(\sum b_{ij}^{2}\right)}{n^{4}}\right)^{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2p)!}{2^{p}p!}w_{n}^{2p}.

Note that
\frac{(2p)!}{2^{p}p!}
is the
2p
-th moment of
N(0,1)
. Hence the proof is completed.∎

Appendix D
Further Simulations

In this section we provide further simulation studies to support the major results provided in the main paper.

D.1
Validity of Theorem
3

Figure
3
shows the histogram of
\frac{\hat{\eta}}{\sqrt{{\rm Var}(\hat{\eta}|\mathcal{F}_{n})}}
from 20000 independent replications constructed using the directed 1-NN graph and the discrete kernel.
Here we take
M=3
and the three distributions are
P_{1}=P_{2}=P_{3}\equiv N(\mathbf{0},I_{d})
with equal sample sizes
n_{i}
and
d=2
.
The red curve is the standard normal density function.
It can be seen from the plots that the empirical distribution is already close to the standard normal distribution for
n_{i}=100
. The approximation gets even better when
n_{i}=1000
, and when
n_{i}=10000
, the empirical distribution is almost identical to the standard normal.

Figure 3:
Histograms of
\frac{\hat{\eta}}{\sqrt{{\rm Var}(\hat{\eta}|\mathcal{F}_{n})}}
as
n_{1}=n_{2}=n_{3}
vary. The red curves are the standard normal density.

D.2
Validation of Theorem
B.3

Here we empirically check the validity of Theorem
B.3
on the detection threshold of our method.

Figure 4:
Empirical power of our method with
n_{1}
samples from
P_{1}=N\left(\mathbf{0},3^{2}\cdot I_{d}\right)
and
n_{2}
samples from
P_{2}=N\left(\mathbf{0},(3+2n^{b})^{2}\cdot I_{d}\right)
.
(n_{1},n_{2})
is taken as either
(2\cdot 10^{4},10^{4})
or
(10^{4},2\cdot 10^{4})
, corresponding to
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})=(\frac{2}{3},\frac{1}{3})
or
(\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3})
. The red vertical line shows the detection threshold predicted by Theorem
B.3
.

Although Theorem
B.3
was proved in the Poissonized setting, our simulation results suggest that the conclusions also hold true in the usual non-Poissonized regime. We consider
M=2
with
n_{1}
samples from
P_{1}=N\left(\mathbf{0},3^{2}\cdot I_{d}\right)
and
n_{2}
samples from

P_{2}=N\left(\mathbf{0},(3+2n^{b})^{2}\cdot I_{d}\right)
for
b\in(-1,0)
,
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
. Here

(n_{1},n_{2})
is taken as either
(2\cdot 10^{4},10^{4})
or
(10^{4},2\cdot 10^{4})
, corresponding to
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})=(\frac{2}{3},\frac{1}{3})
or
(\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3})
.
Figure
4
shows the empirical power of the asymptotic test (
7
) averaged over 200 replications.
The level of the test is set as
\alpha=0.05
and
k
-NN graphs are used with
k=1,2,3
.
The red vertical line shows the detection threshold predicted by Theorem
B.3
.
When
d=5
, the detection threshold is at
n^{-\frac{1}{4}}
. It can be seen that for either choice of
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
,
the power increases from 0.05 to 1 around
b=-\frac{1}{4}
.
For
d=10
and
d=20
, the detection threshold depends on the sign of
a_{k,\theta_{1}}
(see (
B.8
)).
When
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})=(\frac{2}{3},\frac{1}{3})
,
a_{k,\theta_{1}}>0
, and the detection threshold is
n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{d}}
,
so the power increases from 0.05 to 1 around
b=-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{d}
(see the middle and right plots in the first row of Figure
4
).
When
d
is large, this threshold gets closer and closer to the parametric threshold
-\frac{1}{2}
(see the top right plot in Figure
4
).
When
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})=(\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3})
,
a_{k,\theta_{1}}<0
, and the detection threshold is
n^{-\frac{2}{d}}
,
so there is a rapid increase of the power from 0 to 1 around
b=-\frac{2}{d}
. In such a case, the limiting power for
b\in(-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{d},-\frac{2}{d})
is 0 as predicted by Theorem
B.3
, and this is also supported by the last two plots in the second row of Figure
4
, where the empirical power for
b\in(-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{d},-\frac{2}{d})
is close to 0.

D.3
Choice of
k
for the
k
-NN Graph

The choice of
k
for the
k
-NN graph may depend on the task at hand. From the following experiments we see that
for testing the equality of the
M
distributions, the empirical criterion
k=0.10n
, for samples with up to a few hundred observations
[
57
]
, seems to provide a good choice. However, for estimating
\eta
using its empirical version
\hat{\eta}
, a much smaller
k
is recommended — often
k=1
works best.

Choice of
k
in testing
: In Figure
5
we show the empirical power (over 1000 replications) of our test statistics, when the level is set at 0.05, using different
k
-NN graphs.

For
M=3
, the three distributions we consider are:

1.

Normal location problem:
P_{1}=N(\mathbf{0},I_{d})
,
P_{2}=N(0.1\cdot\mathbf{1},I_{d})
,
P_{3}=N(0.2\cdot\mathbf{1},I_{d})
.

2.

Normal scale problem:
P_{1}=N(\mathbf{0},I_{d})
,
P_{2}=N(\mathbf{0},1.5\cdot I_{d})
,
N(\mathbf{0},2\cdot I_{d})
.

3.

Non-Gaussian problem with
t
-distribution:
P_{1}
has each entry following independent
t(1)
with noncentrality parameter
\delta
;
P_{2}
and
P_{3}
have each entry following independent
t(1)
. The dimension
d
is set to be 16.

For
M=5
, the distributions we consider are:

1.

Normal location problem:
P_{1}=N(\mathbf{0},I_{d})
,
P_{2}=N(0.05\cdot\mathbf{1},I_{d})
,
P_{3}=N(0.1\cdot\mathbf{1},I_{d})
,

P_{4}=N(0.15\cdot\mathbf{1},I_{d})
,
P_{5}=N(0.2\cdot\mathbf{1},I_{d})
.

2.

Normal scale problem:
P_{1}=N(\mathbf{0},I_{d})
,
P_{2}=N(\mathbf{0},1.25\cdot I_{d})
,
P_{3}=N(\mathbf{0},1.5\cdot I_{d})
,
P_{4}=N(\mathbf{0},1.75\cdot I_{d})
,
